This calculation responds to equation (11) of the supplied audit: the
inverse-exterior bound for the matrix in WCF6 \cite{WCF}. It constructs
the marked map from the four ES--Fable locales, follows it through the
original quartet and degree-eight conductor, and determines where the
receiving finite linear map can lose rank. All four labels are retained.
The conclusions concern this precise map. They neither assert RH nor
classify all nonlinear maps in the programme.
The original three-dimensional polynomial example was announced by
Levent Alp\"oge with credit to Fable; Tao's exposition \cite{Tao}
reproduces its formula. The additional four-Time constructions used here
are credited to the ES programme source \cite{ES}, rather than attributed
to that original three-point fibre.

The continuation solves the entire inverse problem for the displayed
four-dimensional map: every finite fibre, its complete omitted image,
its degree-eight inverse domain and its complete nonproper-value locus.
It gives the eight signed states over the literal arithmetic quartet,
their common limiting omitted target, and their escape in a fixed
original Gamma norm. For the earlier seven-point target it evaluates
the complete singular spectrum, all exterior inverse asymptotes, and
the exact volume distortion of explicitly transported coordinate flags.
The original conductor intertwines these maps throughout; its coefficients
are never chosen to fit the new polynomial.

The signed-state continuation gives a nonzero exact determinant for the
four sign differences on the literal quartet, the full rank-three mean
map and its explicit kernel line, and the entire eight-state evaluation
kernel. Retaining labels and evaluation gives an invertible total map
through two copies of the original conductor. Its signed-root symmetry
has order $192$; all monodromy generators, its deck transformations,
the complete mixing block and its ranks are calculated explicitly.

\section{Four points, including the coordinates hidden by local symmetry}
The source \cite{ES}, equations \texttt{four-Time-quarter-locales},
\texttt{times-weight-and-unit-vectors}, and
\texttt{four-Time-quarter-coordinate-matrix}, defines
\[
 \mathcal T=(\mathrm M,\mathrm N,\mathrm T,\mathrm E),\qquad
 V=\{v\in\R^4:\mathbf1^Tv=0\},\qquad
 \mu_a=\frac2{\sqrt3}(e_a-\tfrac14\mathbf1),\qquad
 \mathfrak q_a=-\frac13\mu_a.
 \tag{FC1}
\]
Here $e_a$ are the four separate coordinate vectors; no two are identified.
Consequently
\[
 \langle\mathfrak q_a,\mathfrak q_b\rangle
 =\begin{cases}1/9&a=b,\\-1/27&a\ne b,\end{cases}
 \qquad
 Q_{ab}:=\tfrac34\langle\mathfrak q_b,\mu_a\rangle
 =\frac1{12}-\frac13\delta_{ab}.
 \tag{FC2}
\]
The matrix $Q$ has four distinct columns, although each column has its
own local altitude $-1/4$. Direct multiplication gives
\[
 Q\mathbf1=0,\qquad Q|_{\mathbf1^\perp}=-\tfrac13 I,
 \qquad \rank Q=3.
 \tag{FC3}
\]
Thus a specific noninvertible map is already present: simultaneous
altitude readout. Its entire kernel is the constant vector. This is the
source's tetrahedral projection, not a singularity of WCF6.

The affine map which recovers the four indicators is
\[
 b:V_{\C}\longrightarrow\{c\in\C^4:\mathbf1^Tc=1\},\qquad
 b(v)=\tfrac14\mathbf1-\tfrac{3\sqrt3}{2}v,
 \quad b(\mathfrak q_a)=e_a.
 \tag{FC4}
\]
Its inverse is $c\mapsto-2(c-\mathbf1/4)/(3\sqrt3)$.
Both formulas follow by substitution in FC1. They commute with all
simultaneous permutations of the four labels. Retaining the affine term
in FC4 recovers the constant direction that FC3 loses.

\section{Exact transport into the original quartet}
Retain the WCF simple quartet and its full unit, with
$0<\delta<1/2$ and $\gamma>2$. Fix the explicit marking
\[
 \begin{array}{c|cccc}
 a&\mathrm M&\mathrm N&\mathrm T&\mathrm E\\\hline
 \rho_a&\frac12+\delta+i\gamma&\frac12+\delta-i\gamma&
 \frac12-\delta+i\gamma&\frac12-\delta-i\gamma.
 \end{array}
 \tag{FC5}
\]
This is a declared marking, not an inference that the four Time operators
are the arithmetic dilation operator. All other markings are obtained
by simultaneous permutations; their effects on the Segre relation are
calculated below. Put $h(S)=\prod_a(S-\rho_a)$ and
\[
 \ell_a(S)=\prod_{b\ne a}\frac{S-\rho_b}{\rho_a-\rho_b}.
 \tag{FC6}
\]
The exact algebra map is
\[
 \Theta:\operatorname{Map}(\{\mathfrak q_a\},\C)
 \longrightarrow\C[S]/(h),\qquad
 f\longmapsto\sum_a f(\mathfrak q_a)[\ell_a].
 \tag{FC7}
\]
Evaluation at the four $\rho_a$ is its inverse. Indeed
$\ell_a(\rho_b)=\delta_{ab}$; a polynomial of degree at most three
vanishing at all four distinct points is zero. The same argument applied
to remainders modulo $h$ proves
$[\ell_a\ell_b]=\delta_{ab}[\ell_a]$ and
$\sum_a[\ell_a]=1$, so FC7 is an algebra isomorphism, not merely a
vector-space bijection.

For the full original Taylor unit $u_a=(g/h)(\rho_a)\ne0$, the
additional source-unit map is $c\mapsto\diag(u_a)c$, with inverse
$c\mapsto\diag(u_a^{-1})c$. Its determinant is $\prod_a u_a$.
No unit has been replaced by one in the preceding isomorphism or in
the period map below. The unit multiplication is a separate linear
isomorphism, not an unital algebra homomorphism.

The original exponential observation of a coefficient vector is
\[
 \mathcal E(c)(z)=\sum_a c_a e^{\rho_a z},\qquad
 \bigl(\mathcal E(c)^{(n)}(0)\bigr)_{n=0}^3
 =V_\rho c,\quad (V_\rho)_{na}=\rho_a^n.
 \tag{FC8}
\]
The Vandermonde determinant, with precisely the order FC5, is
\[
 \det V_\rho=\prod_{a<b}(\rho_b-\rho_a)
 =-64\delta^2\gamma^2(\delta^2+\gamma^2)\ne0.
 \tag{FC9}
\]
The product formula follows by subtracting columns and induction in the
number of columns; its evaluation uses the six differences
$-2i\gamma,-2\delta,-2\delta-2i\gamma,
-2\delta+2i\gamma,-2\delta,-2i\gamma$.
In particular the four transported points cannot collide under the full
first-four-jet observation. With the unit included the determinant is
FC9 times $\prod_a u_a$.

\section{The degree-eight map is the original conductor map}
Write $t=(t_1,t_2,t_3,t_4)$ in the FC5 order, and retain
\[
 Q_0(t)=t_1t_4-t_2t_3,\qquad
 t(z)=(e^{\rho_1z},e^{\rho_2z},e^{\rho_3z},e^{\rho_4z}).
 \tag{FC10}
\]
Let $\mathcal S_n=\C[t_1,t_2,t_3,t_4]_n$. Substitution along this
curve gives the linear map
\[
 \mathcal E_n:\mathcal S_n\to
 \operatorname{span}\{e^{\beta_{n,u,w}z}:0\le u,w\le n\},
 \quad P\mapsto P(t(z)),\qquad
 \beta_{n,u,w}=\tfrac n2+(2u-n)\delta+i(2w-n)\gamma.
 \tag{FC11}
\]
Its exact kernel is
\[
 \ker\mathcal E_n=Q_0\mathcal S_{n-2},\qquad
 \dim\operatorname{im}\mathcal E_n=(n+1)^2,
 \tag{FC12}
\]
where $\mathcal S_{n-2}=0$ for $n<2$.
To prove this, a monomial $t_1^{k_1}\cdots t_4^{k_4}$ is sent to the
exponential indexed by $u=k_1+k_2,w=k_1+k_3$. Every pair $0\le u,w\le n$
occurs: choose
$k_1=\max(0,u+w-n)$, $k_2=u-k_1$, $k_3=w-k_1$,
$k_4=n-u-w+k_1$. Replacing $t_1t_4$ by $t_2t_3$ reduces any monomial to
this unique representative. Thus the quotient by $Q_0$ has those
$(n+1)^2$ monomial representatives. Their exponents are distinct because
equality of real parts forces $u=u'$ and equality of imaginary parts
forces $w=w'$. Differentiating a linear relation in these exponentials
through order $(n+1)^2-1$ gives a Vandermonde matrix with nonzero
determinant. Hence they are linearly independent and FC12 follows.
At $n=8$, the map has rank $81$ and kernel dimension
$\binom{11}{3}-81=84$.

For the original admitted period let $H$ be its entire invertible period
matrix, including its Gamma factors, branch powers and Taylor unit.
Retain $D_5=\diag(\zeta,\zeta^2,\zeta^3,\zeta^4)$,
$\zeta=e^{2\pi i/5}$, and the precise original polynomials
\[
 Q_j(X)=Q_0(H^{-1}D_5^{-j}X),\qquad
 A_H(X)=\prod_{j=1}^4Q_j(X),\qquad
 E_A(z)=A_H(Ht(z)).
 \tag{FC13}
\]
These are NC5 and DFX4--5 \cite{NC,Cumulative}; no alternative conductor
is being selected. Expanding
$A_H(H(ZW,Z,W,1)^T)=\sum_{u,w=0}^8 a_{uw}Z^uW^w$ gives exactly
\[
 E_A(z)=\sum_{u,w=0}^8a_{uw}e^{\beta_{8,u,w}z},\qquad
 \mu_j=\sum_{u,w=0}^8a_{uw}\beta_{8,u,w}^{\,j}.
 \tag{FC14}
\]
This follows because
$t(z)=e^{(1/2-\delta-i\gamma)z}
(e^{2\delta z}e^{2i\gamma z},e^{2\delta z},e^{2i\gamma z},1)^T$
and $A_H$ is homogeneous of degree eight.

There is also a literal point map into the original quadric:
\[
 \mathfrak q_a\xmapsto{b}e_a\xmapsto{H}He_a,
 \qquad Q_0(e_a)=Q_0(H^{-1}He_a)=0.
\tag{FC15}
\]
The canonical 488-page reader \cite{ES488} also supplies an explicit
four-point polynomial collision in four coordinates, theorem
\texttt{explicit-four-time-Jacobian-collision}.
Its ordered source columns form the matrix
\[
 K=\begin{pmatrix}
 0&i&1/\sqrt2&1/\sqrt2\\
 0&-1&-1-\sqrt2 i&1-\sqrt2 i\\
 i/2&-3i&2\sqrt2+6i&-2\sqrt2+6i\\
 0&13&-34-19\sqrt2 i&34-19\sqrt2 i
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad \det K=77\sqrt2 i/2.
 \tag{FC15a}
\]
Expansion along the first column reduces this determinant to $i/2$
times a three-by-three determinant. Subtracting its second column from
its third gives the column $(0,2,68)^T$; the remaining expansion is
$30\sqrt2+94/\sqrt2=77\sqrt2$, proving the value. Hence the explicit
linear map $x\mapsto HK^{-1}x$, with
$K^{-1}=\operatorname{adj}(K)/(77\sqrt2 i/2)$, sends those four
original collision coordinates to exactly the same four $He_a$ in
FC15. The labelled map from these four source points to the Time locales
is $Ke_a\mapsto\mathfrak q_a$. This supplies a second, fully explicit
source for FC15 without asserting that two different ambient spaces or
their dynamics are identical. The companion independent derivation
records all four polynomial evaluations and their exact certificate.
Put $M_j=H^{-1}D_5^{-j}H$. The exact conductor value at this point is
\[
 A_H(He_a)=\prod_{j=1}^4
 \bigl((M_j)_{1a}(M_j)_{4a}-(M_j)_{2a}(M_j)_{3a}\bigr).
 \tag{FC16}
\]
Its vanishing means that $He_a$ lies on another quadric in the orbit.
It is not the same evaluation as any $\mu_v$: the latter is the
derivative at $z=0$ on the path $Ht(z)$, whose base point is $H\mathbf1$.
Equations FC13--16 give both maps and their different evaluation points
explicitly, rather than discarding the relation between the objects.

More precisely, the four incidences are four coordinates of the
\emph{original} symbol, not an auxiliary symbol. With
$P(t)=A_H(Ht)=\sum_{|m|=8}c_mt^m$, collection in FC11 gives
\[
 a_{u,w}=\sum_{\substack{|m|=8\\m_1+m_2=u,\ m_1+m_3=w}}c_m,
 \quad
 (A_H(He_1),A_H(He_2),A_H(He_3),A_H(He_4))
 =(a_{88},a_{80},a_{08},a_{00}).
 \tag{FC16a}
\]
Indeed each of those four corner pairs admits only the corresponding
pure eighth power. Define
$\mathcal D_\rho=\sum_j\rho_jt_j\partial_{t_j}$. The chain rule,
iterated on each polynomial $\mathcal D_\rho^nP$, proves
\[
 \mu_n=(\mathcal D_\rho^nP)(\mathbf1)
 =\sum_{j=1}^4A_H(He_j)(8\rho_j)^n
  +\sum_{(u,w)\notin\{(8,8),(8,0),(0,8),(0,0)\}}
      a_{uw}\beta_{8,u,w}^{\,n}.
 \tag{FC16b}
\]
Thus all four points enter the exact moment test, with the remaining
77 collected terms explicitly retained. Computing those four incidences
alone does not evaluate the remaining sum. For example, the test
polynomial $t_1^7t_2$ has zero at all four $e_j$ and value one at
$\mathbf1$; this establishes independence of the evaluation functionals,
without substituting that polynomial for the original conductor.

On the original five-orbit domain the quadrics $Q_0(H^{-1}X),Q_1,\ldots,Q_4$
are nonassociate irreducibles. The polynomial $Q_0(t)$ is irreducible:
as a degree-one polynomial in $t_1$ it is primitive over
$\C[t_2,t_3,t_4]$, with coprime coefficients $t_4$ and $-t_2t_3$,
and is irreducible over that coefficient field. An invertible linear
substitution preserves irreducibility. If the degree-eight polynomial
$A_H(Ht)$ belonged to $(Q_0)$, primality would force $Q_0$ to divide
one factor $Q_j(Ht)$, making those degree-two polynomials associates.
This contradicts the original orbit domain. Thus FC12 proves
\[
 E_A\not\equiv0,\qquad
 v=\ord_0E_A\le80,\qquad\mu_v\ne0.
 \tag{FC17}
\]
The order bound follows again from the $81$ distinct exponents and their
first $81$ derivative matrix. It is not a numerical lower bound for the
first nonzero derivative.
More precisely, if $r_A$ is the number of nonzero original collected
coefficients, the same derivative argument gives $v\le r_A-1$.
In particular, vanishing of all four incidences in FC16a leaves
$r_A\le77$ and gives $v\le76$; it does not annihilate the original
nonzero symbol. This support statement evaluates the effect of all four
point-incidence equations on the vanishing-order bound.

\section{All tetrahedral permutations, with the lost equation restored}
The full $S_4$ action on the four labels takes the line $\C Q_0$ to
the three lines represented by
\[
 Q_{14|23}=t_1t_4-t_2t_3,\quad
 Q_{12|34}=t_1t_2-t_3t_4,\quad
 Q_{13|24}=t_1t_3-t_2t_4.
 \tag{FC18}
\]
These correspond to the three partitions into two unordered pairs.
The stabilizer of the first line has order eight (independent exchanges
inside the pairs and exchange of the pairs); it is the dihedral group
of the rectangle. A sign can change when the pairs are exchanged and
is retained by these equations. The three lines are distinct, since
their monomial supports differ. On the unchanged original curve,
\[
 Q_{14|23}(t(z))=0,\quad
 Q_{12|34}(t(z))=2e^z\sinh(2\delta z),\quad
 Q_{13|24}(t(z))=2ie^z\sin(2\gamma z).
 \tag{FC19}
\]
All identities follow by multiplying the original exponentials. The
last two have nonzero first derivatives $4\delta$ and $4i\gamma$ at zero.
Thus equal-looking tetrahedral presentations must carry their pairing
equation as well as their point labels. To transport the whole diagram
under a permutation matrix $P$, use $t'=Pt$, $H'=HP^{-1}$ and
$Q'_0(t')=Q_0(P^{-1}t')$. Then $H't'=Ht$ and
$Q'_0(H'^{-1}X)=Q_0(H^{-1}X)$ exactly; the original conductor is unchanged.
Permuting only labels while silently fixing a different pairing would
instead change its defining equation.

\section{The singularity test in equation (11)}
Retain $d=D+1$, $s\in\{1,a\}$ with $a=4\ell+1\ge9$,
$b=s/2$, and $N=D+v$. WCF2--6 specify the original Gamma polynomial
norms and their orthonormal frames; write
\[
 \rho_n^{(s)}=\sqrt{n!/(b)_n},\qquad
 w(t)=-i\arctan t,\quad
 g(t)=t^{-v}e^{-4w(t)}E_A(w(t)),\quad
 g_0=(-i)^v\mu_v/v!.
 \tag{FC20}
\]
The source and target of the unscaled map are exactly
\[
 T_A:\mathcal P_{D+v}/\mathcal P_{v-1}\to\mathcal P_D,
 \qquad T_AP(S')=\sum_{u,w}a_{uw}P(S'+\beta_{8,u,w}),
 \tag{FC21}
\]
with centers $a/2$ and $a/2-4$ and the WCF2 quotient/target norms.
Its WCF6 matrix and determinant are
\[
 A_{D,s}=\diag(\rho_0^{(s)},\ldots,\rho_D^{(s)})^{-1}
 T_D(g)\diag(\rho_v^{(s)},\ldots,\rho_{D+v}^{(s)}),
 \tag{FC22}
\]
\[
 \det A_{D,s}=\left(\frac{(-i)^v\mu_v}{v!}\right)^{D+1}
 \prod_{j=0}^D\frac{\rho_{j+v}^{(s)}}{\rho_j^{(s)}}\ne0.
 \tag{FC23}
\]
Indeed Taylor expansion gives
$T_AS^n=\sum_{j=v}^n\binom nj\mu_j S'^{n-j}$.
It annihilates precisely $\mathcal P_{v-1}$ and reduces each remaining
leading degree by $v$ with nonzero factor $\binom nv\mu_v$.
Successive elimination proves bijectivity in FC21. In the stated frames
FC22 is triangular; its diagonal proves FC23. Since $b>0$, all displayed
weights are finite and positive. Nothing about the four labelled points
can defeat FC23 while retaining the stated orbit domain and actual
vanishing order.

For completeness, every inverse coefficient is explicitly reconstructed:
\[
 h_0=g_0^{-1},\qquad
 h_n=-g_0^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^ng_jh_{n-j},\qquad
 A_{D,s}^{-1}=\diag(\rho_v^{(s)},\ldots,\rho_{D+v}^{(s)})^{-1}
 T_D(h)\diag(\rho_0^{(s)},\ldots,\rho_D^{(s)}).
 \tag{FC24}
\]
Multiplication of the two truncated Toeplitz matrices gives the identity
by the convolution recurrence, proving this inverse with all factors.

There is a precise rank-drop locus for a \emph{fixed} old quotient.
Keep $v_0$ and $N=D+v_0$ fixed while varying coefficients and shifts
within the conductor formula. It defines
$\mathcal P_N/\mathcal P_{v_0-1}\to\mathcal P_D$ exactly when
$\mu_0=\cdots=\mu_{v_0-1}=0$. Necessity follows by applying the map to
$1,S,\ldots,S^{v_0-1}$ in that order; sufficiency follows from its Taylor
formula. At such a point of finite actual order $v\ge v_0$,
\[
 \rank T_A=\max(D+1-(v-v_0),0),\qquad
 \ker T_A=\mathcal P_{\min(v-1,N)}/\mathcal P_{v_0-1}.
 \tag{FC25}
\]
To prove this, degrees below $v$ vanish and the images of
$S^v,\ldots,S^N$ have different degrees and nonzero leading coefficients;
they span $\mathcal P_{N-v}$ when $N\ge v$. If $E_A=0$ identically the
rank is zero. If actual order is smaller than $v_0$, the old quotient
arrow is undefined, not singular. Restoring the actual kernel and actual
target degree gives FC21 again. Thus a zero of the old leading moment
is an order jump, and the full kernel correction must be retained.

The audit's inverse-exterior expression (11) has singular factors only
when the retained leading moment vanishes or a displayed weight ceases
to be defined. The latter never occurs for the admitted positive $s$;
the former contradicts using the actual order $v$. FC25 states exactly
what a genuine fixed-quotient degeneration would be. No original-period
order-jump point is supplied merely by the four Fable locale coordinates.
The point-conductor incidence test FC16 remains an explicit separate
polynomial calculation in the original $H$, with all four columns retained.

\section{Four explicit inputs to the very operator in equation (11)}
For the programme cutoffs $D\ge3$, the four labels have the following
literal receiving vectors in the WCF source, without introducing another
conductor:
\[
 x_a=[S^v\ell_a(S)]\in\mathcal P_{D+v}/\mathcal P_{v-1},\qquad
 y_a(S')=\sum_{u,w=0}^8 a_{uw}
 (S'+\beta_{8,u,w})^v\ell_a(S'+\beta_{8,u,w}).
 \tag{FC26}
\]
Thus $\mathfrak q_a\mapsto x_a\mapsto y_a=T_Ax_a$ is an explicit
map into equation (11)'s actual operator; the four-dimensional collision
coordinates $Ke_a$ map to the same $x_a$ by first applying $K^{-1}$.
All four output polynomials lie in $\mathcal P_3$.
With the source label basis in FC5 and target monomial basis
$(1,S',S'^2,S'^3)$, their four-column determinant is
\[
 \det\bigl([y_1]\ [y_2]\ [y_3]\ [y_4]\bigr)
 =\frac{\mu_v^4\displaystyle\prod_{j=0}^3\binom{v+j}{v}}
 {-64\delta^2\gamma^2(\delta^2+\gamma^2)}\ne0.
 \tag{FC27}
\]
Here the brackets on the left denote coefficient columns, not an
orthonormal change of the WCF metric. To prove the formula, evaluation
of a cubic at the four roots has matrix $V_\rho^T$. Therefore the
coefficient matrix of the four $\ell_a$ is $(V_\rho^T)^{-1}$.
On the source monomials $S^v,\ldots,S^{v+3}$, Taylor expansion in
FC21 gives a triangular output matrix with diagonal
$\mu_v\binom{v+j}{v}$. Multiplying these two determinants proves
FC27. It also proves all four outputs are linearly independent. The
source norm is the original quotient norm of the classes in FC26;
choosing their minimum-norm representatives only subtracts elements of
$\mathcal P_{v-1}$, which $T_A$ kills exactly. The target norm is the
unchanged original Gamma norm at center $a/2-4$. Thus no modification
of the norms is used to establish the determinant or the images.

The full four-vector Gram determinants can also be evaluated, so the
norm transport need not be left implicit. Set
$\Delta_\rho=-64\delta^2\gamma^2(\delta^2+\gamma^2)$ and retain
\[
 dm_s(y)=\frac{(2\pi)^{s/2}2^{s/2}}{4\pi\Gamma(s/2)}
 |\Gamma(s/4+iy/2)|^2dy,\qquad
 h_{n,s}=(2\pi)^{s/2}n!(s/2)_n.
 \tag{FC28}
\]
Let $G_x=(\langle x_a,x_b\rangle_{s,a/2,\mathrm{quot}})_{ab}$
and $G_y=(\langle y_a,y_b\rangle_{s,a/2-4})_{ab}$. Then
\[
 \det G_x=\frac{\prod_{j=0}^3 h_{v+j,s}}{|\Delta_\rho|^2},\qquad
 \det G_y=\frac{|\mu_v|^8
       \left(\prod_{j=0}^3\binom{v+j}{v}\right)^2
       \prod_{j=0}^3h_{j,s}}{|\Delta_\rho|^2},
 \tag{FC29}
\]
and in particular
\[
 \frac{\det G_y}{\det G_x}
 =\left|\frac{\mu_v}{v!}\right|^8
       \prod_{j=0}^3\frac{(\rho_{v+j}^{(s)})^2}
                              {(\rho_j^{(s)})^2}
 =|\det A_{3,s}|^2>0.
 \tag{FC30}
\]
For the proof, the original orthonormal polynomial of degree $n$ in
$S$ has leading coefficient $i^{-n}/\sqrt{h_{n,s}}$, as in WCF2.
Expanding $S^v,\ldots,S^{v+3}$ in this frame and removing the
degrees below $v$ gives a triangular matrix with diagonal
$i^{v+j}\sqrt{h_{v+j,s}}$. Its Gram determinant is
$\prod_{j=0}^3h_{v+j,s}$. Multiplication by $(V_\rho^T)^{-1}$
therefore proves the first formula in FC29. In the target, the
monomial Gram determinant is $\prod_{j=0}^3h_{j,s}$ by the same
leading-coefficient argument; FC27 proves the second formula.
Dividing the two equalities, substituting $h_{n,s}$ and the binomial
coefficients, proves FC30. Both Gram determinants retain the mass
$(2\pi)^{2s}$; it cancels only in their displayed ratio. This is the
original quotient metric on the four vectors, not a Euclidean norm
imposed on their coefficient columns.

These formulas locate the noninvertibility that survives transport:
FC3 loses the constant direction when reading the four locales together,
and the collision polynomial forgets which marked source was chosen.
FC26 instead retains that source identity through the original
conductor; FC27 excludes a four-point collision or a kernel there.
An attempt to factor FC26's four outputs through a single common image
would make all four columns equal, contradicting FC27. This proves the
failure of that specific factorization with the exact source and target
maps in hand, rather than asserting that the programmes are unrelated.

\section{Transport of the complete nonlinear collision}
In this section and the next two sections, $a$ is the first ES coordinate.
It is separate from the fixed WCF amplification integer, which is now
written $a_{\mathrm{amp}}=4\ell+1\ge9$. Thus the original source and
target centers remain $a_{\mathrm{amp}}/2$ and
$a_{\mathrm{amp}}/2-4$, and $s\in\{1,a_{\mathrm{amp}}\}$.
This dictionary changes no coordinate value or measure.
Here the receiving map is constructed, rather than inferred from a
similarity of determinants. In the original source coordinates
$q=(a,y,z,w)$, the ES reader's four-dimensional polynomial is
\[
\begin{aligned}
 P_0(q)&=a^3z+2a^2y-ia,\\
 P_2(q)&=-a^3y^2z-2ia^2yz+a^2w-2a^2y^3-10iay^2+3az+y,\\
 P_3(q)&=2a^3y^3z+6ia^2y^2z+2a^2wy+4a^2y^4
             +2iaw-4iay^3-2ayz+2iz+7y^2,\\
 P_4(q)&=2a^3y^4z+8ia^2y^3z+a^2wy^2+4a^2y^5
             +2iawy+7iay^4-10ay^2z-4iyz-w-3y^3.
\end{aligned}\tag{FC31}
\]
Write $P=(P_0,P_2,P_3,P_4)$ and $h=(0,0,-1,0)^T$.
Substitution in FC31 gives $P(Ke_a)=h$ for the four columns of FC15a,
and $h=2iKe_{\mathrm M}$. Direct differentiation and determinant
expansion give $\det DP=-2$ identically; the accompanying
\texttt{independent/verify\_four\_labels.py} performs that exact
polynomial calculation and all four evaluations, with rational
coefficients in $\mathbb Q(i,\sqrt2)$.

Let $\mathcal U=\mathcal P_{v+3}/\mathcal P_{v-1}$ and
$\mathcal W=\mathcal P_3$, as subspaces of FC21, and use the original
quotient and target Gamma norms. Define the isomorphisms
\[
 X:\C^4\to\mathcal U,\quad Xe_a=x_a,\qquad
 Y:\C^4\to\mathcal W,\quad Ye_a=y_a,\qquad
 \Phi=XK^{-1},\quad\Psi=YK^{-1}.
 \tag{FC32}
\]
FC27 proves they are isomorphisms and $T_A\Phi=\Psi$.
Consequently the following are explicit polynomial self-maps of the
\emph{actual} original four-dimensional source and target spaces:
\[
 P_{\mathcal U}=\Phi P\Phi^{-1},\qquad
 P_{\mathcal W}=\Psi P\Psi^{-1},\qquad
 T_AP_{\mathcal U}=P_{\mathcal W}T_A.
 \tag{FC33}
\]
The last equality follows by substituting $T_A\Phi=\Psi$ and
$\Phi^{-1}=\Psi^{-1}T_A$ on $\mathcal U$. Both polynomial maps have
constant Jacobian determinant $-2$: the chain rule cancels the two
constant change-of-coordinate determinants. Their norms remain those
of FC28--30. The pulled-back forms on $\C^4$ are, explicitly,
$(K^{-1})^*G_xK^{-1}$ and $(K^{-1})^*G_yK^{-1}$.

In particular the four distinct original target polynomials satisfy
\[
 P_{\mathcal W}(y_{\mathrm M})=
 P_{\mathcal W}(y_{\mathrm N})=
 P_{\mathcal W}(y_{\mathrm T})=
 P_{\mathcal W}(y_{\mathrm E})=2i\,y_{\mathrm M}.
 \tag{FC34}
\]
This is a transported noninjective nonlinear map on the conductor
target. The original linear conductor is the invertible intertwiner
in FC33; it has not acquired a kernel and none of its coefficients
has been fitted to the new map.

No inverse is hidden in these definitions: put
$\ell_a(S)=\sum_{r=0}^3\lambda_{ra}S^r$, where its coefficients are
given by the product FC6. In the target monomial basis the matrix of
$Y$ is exactly
\[
 Y_{ka}=\sum_{r=k}^3\lambda_{ra}\binom{v+r}{k}\mu_{v+r-k},
 \quad 0\le k\le3,\qquad
 P_{\mathcal W}(p)=YK^{-1}P(KY^{-1}[p]).
 \tag{FC35}
\]
Here $[p]$ is the coefficient column of $p$; the last output column is
read in the same monomial basis. The inverses are adjugates divided
by their nonzero determinants in FC15a and FC27. Formula FC35,
together with FC31, is the complete polynomial map in the original
target coordinates, with all original moments retained.

\section{The full fibre contains seven points, with all four labels retained}
The four displayed source points of FC15a are not the entire polynomial
fibre. This does not identify or remove any of the four Time locales.
We now solve every equation $P(q)=h$ over $\C$.

When $a=0$, FC31 reads
\[
 P(q)=(0,y,2iz+7y^2,-4iyz-w-3y^3).
\]
Thus $y=0,z=i/2,w=0$, the single point $q_{\mathrm M}=Ke_{\mathrm M}$.
When $a\ne0$, the equations $P_0=P_2=0$ solve uniquely as
\[
 z=\frac{i-2ay}{a^2},\qquad
 w=\frac{i(7a^2y^2-3iay-3)}{a^3}.
 \tag{FC36}
\]
Substitution into $P_3+1$ and $P_4$ gives exactly
\[
 P_3+1=-\frac{3a^2y^2-a^2+6iay-4}{a^2},\qquad
 P_4=-\frac{(ay+i)(2a^2y^2+4iay-3)}{a^3}.
 \tag{FC37}
\]
Set $t=ay$. Hence
\[
 (t+i)(2t^2+4it-3)=0,\qquad a^2=3t^2+6it-4.
 \tag{FC38}
\]
There are exactly three possible $t$: $-i$ and $-i\pm1/\sqrt2$.
For $t=-i$ the second equation gives $a^2=-1$; for the other two it
gives $a^2=1/2$. In each case both square roots are nonzero, $y=t/a$
is determined, and FC36 determines $z,w$. Thus there are exactly six
points with $a\ne0$ and the one already found with $a=0$.
All implications used above are equivalences, so no elimination root
is extraneous and none is omitted.

The exact involution
\[
 J(a,y,z,w)=(-a,-y,z,-w)
\]
satisfies, term by term in FC31,
\[
 P\circ J=J_{\mathrm{out}}\circ P,\qquad
 J_{\mathrm{out}}(u_0,u_2,u_3,u_4)=(-u_0,-u_2,u_3,-u_4).
 \tag{FC39}
\]
Since $J_{\mathrm{out}}h=h$, the full fibre is
\[
 P^{-1}(h)=\{q_{\mathrm M},q_{\mathrm N},q_{\mathrm T},q_{\mathrm E},
              Jq_{\mathrm N},Jq_{\mathrm T},Jq_{\mathrm E}\}.
 \tag{FC40}
\]
The three additional points, in the unchanged $(a,y,z,w)$ coordinates,
are
\[
\begin{aligned}
 Jq_{\mathrm N}&=(-i,1,-3i,-13),\\
 Jq_{\mathrm T}&=(-1/\sqrt2,1+\sqrt2i,2\sqrt2+6i,34+19\sqrt2i),\\
 Jq_{\mathrm E}&=(-1/\sqrt2,-1+\sqrt2i,-2\sqrt2+6i,-34+19\sqrt2i).
\end{aligned}\tag{FC41}
\]
Their first and second coordinates distinguish all seven, and FC36--38
prove their exhaustiveness.

This entire fibre transports to the original WCF target. Since
$Jq=-q+2z e_z$ and $e_z=-2i q_{\mathrm M}$, its seven points are
\[
\begin{gathered}
 y_{\mathrm M},\ y_{\mathrm N},\ y_{\mathrm T},\ y_{\mathrm E},\quad
 -12y_{\mathrm M}-y_{\mathrm N},\\
 (24-8\sqrt2i)y_{\mathrm M}-y_{\mathrm T},\quad
 (24+8\sqrt2i)y_{\mathrm M}-y_{\mathrm E}.
\end{gathered}\tag{FC42}
\]
They are precisely $P_{\mathcal W}^{-1}(2iy_{\mathrm M})$, by the
bijective conjugacy FC33. Every one is a locally invertible point of
$P_{\mathcal W}$ because its Jacobian determinant is $-2$.
The failure is a global inverse at the common target, not a zero of
the local derivative or of the linear conductor determinant.

\section{The explicit escaping branch and its original norm}
There is also a rational symmetry which leaves every output unchanged.
On the exact domain $a\ne0$, set
\[
 \Sigma(a,y,z,w)=\left(-a,\ y+\frac{2i}{a},\
 \frac{6i}{a^2}-z,\
 w-\frac{14iy^2}{a}+\frac{28y}{a^2}+\frac{40i}{a^3}\right).
 \tag{FC43}
\]
Direct substitution gives $\Sigma^2=I$ and $P\Sigma=P$ on this
domain. One can verify the latter identity with fewer terms in the
reader's original factor coordinates:
\[
\begin{aligned}
 b&=i+ay,&c&=-i+2ay+a^2z,\\
 d&=-iy-a(iz+2y^2)-a^2yz,&e&=2z-7iy^2+aw,\\
 f&=iw+3iy^3-4yz+a(6iy^2z+wy+4y^4)+2a^2y^3z.
\end{aligned}\tag{FC44}
\]
Substitution of FC43 into these formulas changes
$(a,b,c,d,e,f)$ to $(-a,-b,-c,-d,-e,-f)$.
The polynomial outputs are $(ac,ae+bd,af+be,bf)$, so all four
remain unchanged. The first four solved coordinates also show the
sign operation is an involution; alternatively it follows by inserting
FC43 twice. For $a=0$, the chart fixes $b=i$. The negative of the
$\mathrm M$ factor state has $b=-i$ and lies outside this chart.
The other three factor-state negatives are present among FC41.
This records the orientation data without treating an opposite
factorization as an identical labelled point.

The exact factor count explains why four labels coexist with seven
points in this affine fibre. A marked factorization of
$H(U,V)=UV(U-V)(U+V)$ chooses one of its four distinct linear factors
as $L=aU+bV$, and places the remaining factors in
$C=cU^3+dU^2V+eUV^2+fV^3$. The resultant is
\[
 R(L,C)=C(-b,a)=a^3f-a^2be+ab^2d-b^3c.
 \tag{FC44a}
\]
For each marked root it is nonzero, because none of the remaining
three factors vanishes on that root. Replacing $(L,C)$ by
$(\lambda L,\lambda^{-1}C)$ changes this resultant by $\lambda^2$.
Thus $R=1$ has exactly two scalar choices for each of the four
marked roots, eight factor states in all. Their signs are distinct.
All have $ad+bc=1$, the $U^3V$ coefficient of $H$.

On $a\ne0$, FC44 has the exact inverse
\[
 y=(b-i)/a,\qquad z=(c+i-2ay)/a^2,\qquad
 w=(e-2z+7iy^2)/a.
 \tag{FC44b}
\]
The incidence equation $ad+bc=1$ then determines $d$ uniquely, and
FC44a with $R=1$ determines $f$ uniquely. This proves that all six
factor states with $a\ne0$ occur in the chart. When $a=0$, those two
incidence equations force $bc=1$ and $-b^3c=1$, hence $b^2=-1$.
The chart FC44 has $b=i$ at $a=0$; its remaining coordinates are
recovered by $y=id$, $z=(e+7iy^2)/2$ and
$w=(f-3iy^3+4yz)/i$. It contains the entire $b=i$ component and none
of the $b=-i$ component. At the particular product $H$, the omitted
state is $(a,b,c,d,e,f)=(0,-i,i,0,-i,0)$. This proves precisely that
the chosen chart retains seven of the eight signed factor states,
still over the same four marked roots. No Time label has been added
or discarded.

Take real $\epsilon>0$ and the two explicit source curves
\[
 q_\epsilon=(\epsilon,0,i/2,0),\qquad
 \widehat q_\epsilon=\Sigma q_\epsilon
 =(-\epsilon,2i/\epsilon,6i/\epsilon^2-i/2,40i/\epsilon^3).
 \tag{FC45}
\]
The latter escapes to infinity whereas the former tends to
$q_{\mathrm M}$. Their identical images are
\[
 P(q_\epsilon)=P(\widehat q_\epsilon)
 =(-i\epsilon+i\epsilon^3/2,\ 3i\epsilon/2,\ -1,\ 0)
 \longrightarrow h.
 \tag{FC46}
\]
Thus $h$ is an explicit nonproper value of the nonlinear map: a
sequence escaping every compact subset has convergent images.
No numerical search is needed.

The same statement holds in the original target norm, with exact
constants. Put $z_\epsilon=\Psi\widehat q_\epsilon$ and
\[
 R=\Psi e_w=
 \frac6{77}y_{\mathrm M}+\frac1{77}y_{\mathrm N}
 +\frac{-3-\sqrt2i}{154}y_{\mathrm T}
 +\frac{3-\sqrt2i}{154}y_{\mathrm E}.
 \tag{FC47}
\]
The displayed coefficients are the last column of $K^{-1}$, verified
by multiplying by FC15a. They are not all zero, so $R\ne0$ by FC27.
Multiplying FC45 by $\epsilon^3\Psi$ gives the norm-convergent limit
\[
 \epsilon^3 z_\epsilon\longrightarrow40iR,\qquad
 \epsilon^3\|z_\epsilon\|_{s,a_{\mathrm{amp}}/2-4}
 \longrightarrow40\|R\|_{s,a_{\mathrm{amp}}/2-4}>0.
 \tag{FC48}
\]
The limit uses only a fixed finite-dimensional linear map and the
original positive Gamma norm. Simultaneously
\[
 P_{\mathcal W}(z_\epsilon)
 =\Psi(-i\epsilon+i\epsilon^3/2,3i\epsilon/2,-1,0)
 \longrightarrow2iy_{\mathrm M}.
 \tag{FC49}
\]
Equations FC42 and FC45--49 therefore identify the full finite fibre
and a branch escaping to infinity above its common value in the
actual conductor target. They do not make that introduced nonlinear
map a linear conductor or an arithmetic dilation action. The exact
relation to the original conductor is the intertwining square FC33,
which preserves its inverse bound.

\section{Compatibility with the original translation action}
The nonlinear transport can be tested against an actual symmetry of the
original conductor. It is not enough to have a conjugate polynomial
on its target. The original finite-shift formula FC21 satisfies
\[
 T_A\partial_S=\partial_{S'}T_A,\qquad
 T_A(P(S+t))=(T_AP)(S'+t).
 \tag{FC50}
\]
Both identities follow by differentiating, respectively translating,
each term of the unchanged finite sum. Differentiation preserves
$\mathcal P_{v-1}$ and therefore descends on $\mathcal U$.
In the source basis $[S^v],\ldots,[S^{v+3}]$ and target basis
$1,S',S'^2,S'^3$, its matrices are
\[
 N_v=\begin{pmatrix}0&v+1&0&0\\0&0&v+2&0\\0&0&0&v+3\\0&0&0&0\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad
 N_0=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0&0\\0&0&2&0\\0&0&0&3\\0&0&0&0\end{pmatrix}.
 \tag{FC51}
\]
Let $B_v$ have entries
$(B_v)_{kj}=\binom{v+j}{k}\mu_{v+j-k}$ for $k\le j$ and zero
otherwise. FC35 gives $Y=B_v(V_\rho^T)^{-1}$, and FC50 gives
$N_0B_v=B_vN_v$. Thus the complete original translation matrix
in the Fable coordinates is exactly
\[
 M=\Psi^{-1}N_0\Psi
   =K V_\rho^T N_v(V_\rho^T)^{-1}K^{-1},\qquad M^4=0,
 \quad M^3\ne0.
 \tag{FC52}
\]
No moment has been set to one in this formula: their cancellation is
the proved identity $N_0B_v=B_vN_v$. They remain in $\Psi$ and in
the native metrics.

The quotient correction has an explicit form in the labelled basis.
Writing $R_v=V_\rho^T N_v(V_\rho^T)^{-1}$ gives
\[
 (R_v)_{ba}=\ell_a'(\rho_b)
          +v\frac{\delta_{ba}-\ell_a(0)}{\rho_b}.
 \tag{FC53}
\]
Indeed, modulo $\mathcal P_{v-1}$,
\[
 \partial_S(S^vf(S))
 =S^v\left(f'(S)+v\frac{f(S)-f(0)}S\right).
\]
The removed term is exactly $vf(0)S^{v-1}$, and the quotient in
parentheses is a polynomial. Evaluating at the four nonzero $\rho_b$
proves FC53. It retains the term that would be lost by treating the
quotient as an ordinary cubic space before transporting its action.

The introduced nonlinear map does not preserve this original
translation flow. From FC31,
\[
 P(0)=0,\qquad J_0:=DP(0)=\diag(-i,1,2i,-1).
 \tag{FC54}
\]
All four eigenvalues of $J_0$ are distinct. A matrix commuting with
this diagonal matrix must be diagonal, since its $(j,k)$ entry is
multiplied in the commutator by the nonzero difference of the two
eigenvalues whenever $j\ne k$. If $M$ commuted with $J_0$, it
would consequently be diagonal; $M^4=0$ would then force $M=0$,
contradicting $M^3\ne0$. Hence $J_0M-MJ_0\ne0$.

More strongly, $P e^{tM}=e^{tM}P$ fails as a polynomial identity
for every $t\ne0$ in $\C$. Otherwise differentiation at zero
would make $J_0$ commute with $R=e^{tM}$. Since $(R-I)^4=0$,
\[
 M=t^{-1}\left((R-I)-\tfrac12(R-I)^2+\tfrac13(R-I)^3\right).
\]
This identity follows by substituting
$R=I+tM+t^2M^2/2+t^3M^3/6$ and collecting powers of $M$.
It would force $J_0$ to commute with $M$, the contradiction just
proved. Conjugating by $\Psi$ proves the same failure for
$P_{\mathcal W}$ and the original target translations $e^{tN_0}$.

The entire correction is explicit, rather than an unspecified failure:
\[
 \Delta(q):=DP(q)Mq-MP(q),\qquad
 P(e^{tM}q)-e^{tM}P(q)
 =\int_0^t e^{(t-s)M}\Delta(e^{sM}q)\,ds.
 \tag{FC55}
\]
For complex $t$, integrate on the straight segment from zero to $t$.
Differentiating $e^{(t-s)M}P(e^{sM}q)$ proves FC55 by the
fundamental theorem of calculus. Every exponential truncates after
its cubic term, so the integrand and integral are finite explicit
polynomials. The linear part of $\Delta$ is precisely
$(J_0M-MJ_0)q\ne0$. The original-target correction is
$\Psi$ times the right side of FC55, with $q=\Psi^{-1}p$;
its norm is evaluated in the unchanged form
$(K^{-1})^*G_yK^{-1}$.

Thus FC33 is a complete nonlinear intertwining with the original
conductor, FC42 is its complete seven-point collision, FC48--49
is its exact nonproper branch, and FC55 is its nonzero correction
for the conductor's original translation action. None of these
calculations identifies this additional nonlinear map with the
arithmetic dilation action or makes the inverse-exterior bound false.

% BEGIN ESCAPE_DIFFERENTIAL
\section{Every exterior inverse along the escaping branch, in the original metric}
The original branch FC45 yields an exact pointwise differential calculation,
not only an unbounded sequence. Throughout this section $\epsilon>0$, the
quartet, original conductor, $a_{\mathrm{amp}}$, and $s$ are fixed. Let
$\widehat q_\epsilon$ be FC45, $z_\epsilon=\Psi\widehat q_\epsilon$,
and retain the full positive matrix
\[
 G=(K^{-1})^*G_yK^{-1},\qquad
 \|q\|_G^2=q^*Gq=\|\Psi q\|_{s,a_{\mathrm{amp}}/2-4}^2.
 \tag{FC56}
\]
No entry of this form is replaced by a Euclidean coordinate norm.
Its entries can be obtained by a finite formula in the original moments,
so FC56 does not leave an unspecified metric to be chosen. Write
$F=YK^{-1}$ in the target monomial basis, $c=a_{\mathrm{amp}}/2-4$,
$b=s/2$, and $M_s=(2\pi)^{s/2}$. Then
\[
 G=F^*\mathcal H F,\qquad
 \mathcal H_{jk}=\sum_{p=0}^j\sum_{q=0}^k
 \binom jp\binom kq c^{j+k-p-q}(-i)^p i^q m_{p+q},
 \quad 0\le j,k\le3,
 \tag{FC56a}
\]
where $m_0=M_s$, $m_2=M_sb$, $m_4=M_s(3b^2+2b)$,
$m_6=M_s(15b^3+30b^2+16b)$, and the odd moments vanish.
To prove this formula, expand
$(c-iy)^j(c+iy)^k$ in the defining measure FC28. The measure is
even. Integrating the WCF generating function in WCF3, cited in
\cite{WCF}, leaves only its constant orthogonal-polynomial coefficient,
so its moment generating function at $u=\arctan t$ is
$\int e^{uy}\,dm_s(y)=M_s(\cos u)^{-b}$ near zero. The coefficients
through order six give exactly the stated moments by differentiation.
The identity can equivalently be read coefficientwise through degree
six, using orthogonality, so no exchange of an infinite series outside
its convergence domain is needed. FC35 specifies every entry of $F$
in $\mu_v,\ldots,\mu_{v+3}$ and the unchanged quartet.
Differentiating the polynomial FC31 and substituting FC45 gives
\[
 J_\epsilon=DP(\widehat q_\epsilon)=
 \begin{pmatrix}
 9i-3i\epsilon^2/2&2\epsilon^2&-\epsilon^3&0\\
 -7i/2-30i/\epsilon^2&\epsilon^2-27&-3\epsilon&\epsilon^2\\
 -2/\epsilon+104/\epsilon^3&-i\epsilon-56i/\epsilon&-2i&2i\epsilon\\
 -4i/\epsilon^2+64i/\epsilon^4&2+28/\epsilon^2&0&-1
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad \det J_\epsilon=-2.
 \tag{FC57}
\]
Thus $D P_{\mathcal W}(z_\epsilon)=\Psi J_\epsilon\Psi^{-1}$.
The inverse matrix, including all its lower-order terms, is
\[
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.25}
\begin{pmatrix}
 \frac{3i\epsilon^2}{2}-i&-\frac{3i\epsilon^4}{2}+2i\epsilon^2&
 \frac{3\epsilon^5}{2}-\frac{5\epsilon^3}{2}&\frac{3i\epsilon^6}{2}-3i\epsilon^4\\
 -\frac32+\frac2{\epsilon^2}&\frac{3\epsilon^2}{2}-3&
 \frac{3i\epsilon^3}{2}-\frac{7i\epsilon}{2}&-\frac{3\epsilon^4}{2}+4\epsilon^2\\
 \frac{9\epsilon}{4}-\frac{18}{\epsilon}+\frac{12}{\epsilon^3}&
 -\frac{9\epsilon^3}{4}+\frac{39\epsilon}{2}-\frac{24}{\epsilon}&
 -\frac{9i\epsilon^4}{4}+\frac{81i\epsilon^2}{4}-\frac{59i}{2}&
 \frac{9\epsilon^5}{4}-21\epsilon^3+35\epsilon\\
 3-\frac{138}{\epsilon^2}+\frac{120}{\epsilon^4}&
 -3\epsilon^2+140-\frac{212}{\epsilon^2}&
 -3i\epsilon^3+141i\epsilon-\frac{258i}{\epsilon}&
 3\epsilon^4-142\epsilon^2+303
\end{pmatrix}.
\tag{FC58}
\]
This is $J_\epsilon^{-1}$: multiplying FC57 by FC58 gives the identity
entry by entry. It is also $-\operatorname{adj}(J_\epsilon)/2$.
These two exact checks, rather than numerical singular-value sampling,
are recorded by \texttt{derive\_escape\_differential.py}.

For exterior powers use increasing coordinate subsets in the order
$1=a,2=y,3=z,4=w$. Let $E^{(r)}_{I,J}$ send the coordinate wedge
$e_J$ to $e_I$ and annihilate the other coordinate wedges. Expanding
the minors of FC57 proves all four finite matrix limits
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c}
 r&m_r&\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow0}
       \epsilon^{m_r}\wedge^rJ_\epsilon&
 \displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow0}
       \epsilon^{m_r}\wedge^rJ_\epsilon^{-1}\\\hline
 1&4&64iE^{(1)}_{4,1}&120E^{(1)}_{4,1}\\
 2&5&-672E^{(2)}_{34,12}&336E^{(2)}_{34,12}\\
 3&4&240E^{(3)}_{234,123}&32iE^{(3)}_{234,123}\\
 4&0&-2&-1/2
\end{array}.
\tag{FC59}
\]
For explicit verification, each exterior matrix has entries
$\det(J_{I,J})$ or $\det((J^{-1})_{I,J})$. Their minimum Laurent
exponents are respectively $-4,-5,-4,0$; the coefficients at those
exponents are exactly the displayed single-entry matrices. Every
remaining monomial has larger exponent. This proves the limits and
the absence of any more singular omitted entry. The accompanying
script expands every minor and checks the limits symbolically over
$\mathbb Q(i)(\epsilon)$.

Here are the exact constants in the original norm. For a principal
submatrix write $G_{I,I}$; inverses below are of the full matrix $G$.
Set
\[
 \tau_1=\sqrt{G_{44}(G^{-1})_{11}},\qquad
 \tau_2=\sqrt{\det G_{34,34}\det(G^{-1})_{12,12}}
       =\frac{\det G_{34,34}}{\sqrt{\det G}}>0.
 \tag{FC60}
\]
Both constants are strictly positive because $G$ is positive definite.
For the original wedge metric, the squared norm of $e_I$ is
$\det G_{I,I}$ and that of its coordinate dual functional $e_J^*$ is
$\det(G^{-1})_{J,J}$. The norm of a rank-one map $x\lambda$ is
$\|x\|\|\lambda\|$: the inequality is Cauchy--Schwarz for the
Riesz representative of $\lambda$, and equality is attained at that
representative. It follows that the rank-one norms in FC59 are
$64\tau_1$, $672\tau_2$, and $240\tau_1$.
For the last equality use
\[
 \det G_{234,234}=\det G\,(G^{-1})_{11},\quad
 \det(G^{-1})_{123,123}=G_{44}/\det G.
\]
These complementary-minor formulas follow from the cofactor inverse
formula for three-element minors. For the two-element formula in
FC60, block-eliminate the positive principal block $G_{34,34}$.
The complementary block of $G^{-1}$ is the inverse Schur complement,
and $\det G$ is the product of that complement's determinant with
$\det G_{34,34}$, giving the claimed identity without discarding
any cross term.

Continuity of the norm of a finite matrix with fixed positive domain
and codomain forms now proves
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
r&\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow0}\epsilon^{m_r}
 \|\wedge^r DP_{\mathcal W}(z_\epsilon)\|&
\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow0}\epsilon^{m_r}
 \|\wedge^r DP_{\mathcal W}(z_\epsilon)^{-1}\|\\\hline
1&64\tau_1&120\tau_1\\
2&672\tau_2&336\tau_2\\
3&240\tau_1&32\tau_1\\
4&2&1/2
\end{array}.
\tag{FC61}
\]
Every norm here is in the original target Gamma metric and its induced
exterior metric. The pulled-back matrix $G$ is only its exact coordinate
expression; the maps $\Psi$ and $K$ have not been declared unitary.

Let $\sigma_1(\epsilon)\ge\cdots\ge\sigma_4(\epsilon)>0$
be the four singular values of this derivative in that metric. The
largest singular value of $\wedge^r J$ is the product of its largest
$r$ singular values: diagonalizing the two positive singular-value
factors gives the wedge basis with all $r$-fold products. Taking
successive ratios of FC61 therefore proves the full spectrum
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \sigma_1(\epsilon)&\sim64\tau_1\epsilon^{-4},&
 \sigma_2(\epsilon)&\sim\frac{21\tau_2}{2\tau_1}\epsilon^{-1},\\
 \sigma_3(\epsilon)&\sim\frac{5\tau_1}{14\tau_2}\epsilon,&
 \sigma_4(\epsilon)&\sim\frac{1}{120\tau_1}\epsilon^4.
\end{aligned}\tag{FC62}
\]
The product of the four displayed constants is $2$, agreeing with
the exact determinant modulus. In particular the condition number
is asymptotic to $7680\tau_1^2\epsilon^{-8}$. The degeneration is
at infinity along a proved branch; each finite point still has
nonzero Jacobian determinant.

There is also an exact inverse derivative identity in the direction
of the image curve. Differentiating FC45--46 gives
\[
 J_\epsilon
 \begin{pmatrix}-1\\-2i\epsilon^{-2}\\-12i\epsilon^{-3}\\-120i\epsilon^{-4}\end{pmatrix}
 =\begin{pmatrix}-i+3i\epsilon^2/2\\3i/2\\0\\0\end{pmatrix}.
 \tag{FC63}
\]
This identifies a specific bounded target velocity whose inverse
grows as $\epsilon^{-4}$, in addition to the full singular spectrum.
If $p_\epsilon=P_{\mathcal W}(z_\epsilon)$, put
$v_0=\Psi(-i,3i/2,0,0)^T\ne0$ and $p_*=2iy_{\mathrm M}$.
FC46 implies $(p_\epsilon-p_*)/\epsilon\to v_0$. Combining
this limit with FC48 and FC61 gives the exact distance laws
\[
 \|p_\epsilon-p_*\|^3\|z_\epsilon\|
       \longrightarrow40\|\Psi e_w\|\|v_0\|^3,
 \qquad
 \|p_\epsilon-p_*\|^4
 \|DP_{\mathcal W}(z_\epsilon)^{-1}\|
       \longrightarrow120\tau_1\|v_0\|^4.
 \tag{FC64}
\]
All norms and all constants in these two limits are the same original
Gamma norms of FC56. Injectivity of $\Psi$ proves their positivity.

\section{The exact return to audit equation (11)}
For an invertible map $B$ of a four-dimensional Hilbert space,
its singular values prove
\[
 \|\wedge^r B^{-1}\|=
       \frac{\|\wedge^{4-r}B\|}{|\det B|}.
 \tag{FC65}
\]
Applying this to FC57 gives precisely the inverse constants in FC61,
including $\|\wedge^2 B^{-1}\|=\|\wedge^2B\|/2$.
Thus the escaping branch realizes highly anisotropic distortion while
satisfying the same exterior identity used to derive audit (11).
Its forward bound is not fixed along the branch: it grows with the
explicit poles in FC59. No contradiction with that identity occurs.

The exact relation to the original conductor is stronger than applying
the identity to an unrelated matrix. Put $L=T_A|_{\mathcal U}$,
$u_\epsilon=\Phi\widehat q_\epsilon$, and
$B_{U,\epsilon}=DP_{\mathcal U}(u_\epsilon)$,
$B_{W,\epsilon}=DP_{\mathcal W}(z_\epsilon)$. Differentiating FC33
at the very same source point proves
\[
 LB_{U,\epsilon}=B_{W,\epsilon}L,\qquad
 B_{W,\epsilon}^{-1}=L B_{U,\epsilon}^{-1}L^{-1}.
 \tag{FC66}
\]
This identity retains the original source quotient metric on $\mathcal U$
and target metric on $\mathcal W$. In particular, for every $0\le r\le4$,
\[
 \frac{\|\wedge^r B_{U,\epsilon}^{-1}\|}
 {\|\wedge^r L\|\,\|\wedge^r L^{-1}\|}
 \le\|\wedge^r B_{W,\epsilon}^{-1}\|
 \le\|\wedge^r L\|\,\|\wedge^r L^{-1}\|
       \|\wedge^r B_{U,\epsilon}^{-1}\|.
 \tag{FC67}
\]
The upper bound follows from composition of exterior maps; the lower
bound follows by solving FC66 for $B_{U,\epsilon}^{-1}$. On the four-plane,
$L$ is exactly $A_{3,s}$ in the WCF orthonormal frames, so the actual
bound in audit (11) applies to the unchanged factor
$\|\wedge^rL^{-1}\|$. For fixed conductor this factor is independent
of $\epsilon$. All four pole orders therefore survive transport through
the original conductor in both directions. This supplies a quantitative
return to the audited map, without assigning to it the nonlinear map's
variable derivative or removing a kernel without its quotient.

For completeness, define the source pullback form
$G_U=(K^{-1})^*G_xK^{-1}$. Replacing $G$ in FC60 by $G_U$ proves
the exact source constants in FC61--64 as well, since the Laurent
matrices FC57--59 are unchanged. Both source and target degenerations
are now evaluated in their original norms. The constant determinant
and the nonproper inverse are compatible; the first does not control
the separate singular values, which FC62 evaluates completely.

\section{The singular flags and the exact volumes of fixed Fable flags}
The compound limits determine the directions of this distortion as well
as its size. Let $I_k=\{1,\ldots,k\}$ and
$J_k=\{5-k,\ldots,4\}$. For $k=1,2,3$, the limiting right
singular $k$-plane in the $G$ coordinates and the limiting left plane are
\[
 \mathcal R_k=G^{-1}\operatorname{span}\{e_j:j\in I_k\},\qquad
 \mathcal L_k=\operatorname{span}\{e_j:j\in J_k\}.
 \tag{FC68}
\]
To prove this, the rank-one compound limit in FC59 has image line
$\C e_{J_k}$ and covector proportional to $e_{I_k}^*$.
The Riesz vector of the coordinate covector $e_j^*$ in the original
form is $G^{-1}e_j$, because
$\langle G^{-1}e_j,x\rangle_G=e_j^*x$.
Taking wedges gives the right singular line
$\C\bigwedge_{j\in I_k}(G^{-1}e_j)$. The nonzero singular value
of each limiting rank-one map is simple; its other singular values
are zero. For convergent matrices with this property, their leading
left and right singular lines converge: a convergent subsequence of
unit maximizing vectors must attain the limiting positive norm and
therefore belongs to that unique line. Compactness of the unit sphere
and uniqueness give convergence of the full family of lines. The
Pl\"ucker line of a decomposable nonzero wedge determines its plane
by $x\wedge\omega=0$, so the singular $k$-planes have the stated
limits. Applying $\Psi$ gives these planes in the literal original
target, with its unchanged Gamma metric.

In particular, the greatest right-singular direction tends to
$\C G^{-1}e_1$, and the greatest left-singular direction tends to
$\C e_4$. Applying the inverse compound limit in FC59 shows that
the least right-singular direction tends to $\C e_4$, and its
least left-singular direction tends to $\C G^{-1}e_1$.
These are directions, not a declaration that the coordinate vectors
are orthogonal or of unit length.

There is also a complete calculation for fixed, explicitly transported
subspaces, as in the audit's endpoint Gram formula (12). Let
\[
 E_k=\Psi\operatorname{span}\{e_1,\ldots,e_k\}\subset\mathcal W,
 \quad 1\le k\le4,
 \qquad (d_1,d_2,d_3,d_4)=(64,672,240,2).
\]
The ratio of the output Gram determinant of this particular frame under
$B_{W,\epsilon}$ to its input Gram determinant is exactly
\[
 \mathcal V_k(\epsilon)=
 \frac{\det\bigl((J_\epsilon^*GJ_\epsilon)_{I_k,I_k}\bigr)}
      {\det G_{I_k,I_k}},\qquad
 \epsilon^{2m_k}\mathcal V_k(\epsilon)
 \longrightarrow d_k^2\frac{\det G_{J_k,J_k}}{\det G_{I_k,I_k}}>0.
 \tag{FC69}
\]
Indeed the numerator is the squared original wedge norm of
$(\wedge^kJ_\epsilon)e_{I_k}$. FC59 takes this wedge, after
the displayed power of $\epsilon$, to a scalar of modulus $d_k$
times $e_{J_k}$. Taking its squared norm proves FC69, while the
denominator is the exact squared norm of the input wedge. Hence
\[
 \log\mathcal V_k(\epsilon)=2m_k\log(1/\epsilon)
   +\log\left(d_k^2\frac{\det G_{J_k,J_k}}
                                {\det G_{I_k,I_k}}\right)+o(1).
 \tag{FC70}
\]
For $k=4$, this ratio is identically $4$, not merely asymptotic to it.
For $k=1,2,3$ it diverges with the specified rates, even though the
full determinant stays fixed. This computes actual subspaces and their
exact original metric constants; it is not an upper enclosure for an
unspecified endpoint. The $E_k$ are the transported Fable coordinate
flags defined here, and no equality with a different programme endpoint
is inferred. Their source partners $\Phi\operatorname{span}(e_1,\ldots,e_k)$
are carried to them by the original $L$ in FC66. Replacing $G$ by
$G_U$ gives their full source volume formulas by the same proof.

The difference between the actual coordinate-flag constant and the
optimizing singular-flag constant is itself exact. For $k=1,2,3$,
FC59--60 and FC69 give
\[
 \lim_{\epsilon\downarrow0}
 \frac{\|\wedge^k B_{W,\epsilon}\|^2}{\mathcal V_k(\epsilon)}
 =\det G_{I_k,I_k}\det(G^{-1})_{I_k,I_k}\ge1,
 \tag{FC71}
\]
with equality precisely when $G_{I_k,I_k^c}=0$. To prove this
without omitting the cross terms, write $G$ in these blocks as
$\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A&C\\C^*&D\end{smallmatrix}\right)$.
The corresponding inverse block is $(A-CD^{-1}C^*)^{-1}$.
The matrix $A^{-1/2}(A-CD^{-1}C^*)A^{-1/2}$ is positive and at
most the identity, since $CD^{-1}C^*$ is positive semidefinite.
Its determinant is at most one, which proves the inequality.
Equality makes all its eigenvalues one, hence $CD^{-1}C^*=0$.
Positive definiteness of $D^{-1}$ then gives $C=0$; the reverse
implication follows by substitution. The limit equality follows
by dividing the rank-one operator constant
$d_k^2\det G_{J_k,J_k}\det(G^{-1})_{I_k,I_k}$ by the fixed-frame
constant in FC69. Thus the exact preserved cross Gram determines
the slack in replacing these particular flag volumes by the operator
bound used in audit (11).
% END ESCAPE_DIFFERENTIAL

% BEGIN GLOBAL_FIBRE
\providecommand{\cP}{\mathcal P}
% Full independent global proof reviewed and integrated by root.
The following complete inverse calculation extends the canonical ES source
\cite{ES488}, whose original polynomial and four marked representatives
are retained. It classifies every target, then specializes the result
to the literal four roots used by the original conductor.

\section{The original polynomial and the factor coordinates}
Fix $i^2=-1$. The original coordinates are $(a,y,z,w)\in\C^4$,
and the ordered output coordinates are $(u_0,u_2,u_3,u_4)$. Define
\begin{align}
 P_0={}&a^3z+2a^2y-ia,\nonumber\\
 P_2={}&-a^3y^2z-2ia^2yz+a^2w-2a^2y^3-10iay^2+3az+y,\nonumber\\
 P_3={}&2a^3y^3z+6ia^2y^2z+2a^2wy+4a^2y^4+2iaw
       -4iay^3-2ayz+2iz+7y^2,\nonumber\\
 P_4={}&2a^3y^4z+8ia^2y^3z+a^2wy^2+4a^2y^5+2iawy
       +7iay^4-10ay^2z-4iyz-w-3y^3.
 \tag{GF1}
\end{align}
The factor-coordinate map $\Phi_{\mathrm{fac}}(a,y,z,w)=(a,b,c,d,e,f)$ is
\begin{align}
 b&=i+ay,&c&=-i+2ay+a^2z,\nonumber\\
 d&=-iy-a(iz+2y^2)-a^2yz,&e&=2z-7iy^2+aw,\nonumber\\
 f&=iw+3iy^3-4yz+a(6iy^2z+wy+4y^4)+2a^2y^3z.
 &&\tag{GF2}
\end{align}
Let
\[
 L=aU+bV,\qquad C=cU^3+dU^2V+eUV^2+fV^3.
\]
Their original incidence and resultant equations are
\begin{equation}
 ad+bc=1,\qquad
 R(L,C)=C(-b,a)=a^3f-a^2be+ab^2d-b^3c=1.\tag{GF3}
\end{equation}
Direct substitution of GF2 proves both identities. Multiplication
of the two factors gives the complete coefficient map
\begin{equation}
 LC=(ac)U^4+U^3V+(ae+bd)U^2V^2+(af+be)UV^3+(bf)V^4,
 \qquad P=(ac,ae+bd,af+be,bf).\tag{GF4}
\end{equation}
Substitution of GF2 in GF4 gives precisely GF1.

On $a\ne0$, every factor state satisfying GF3 has the unique inverse
\begin{equation}
 y=(b-i)/a,\qquad z=(c+i-2ay)/a^2,\qquad
 w=(e-2z+7iy^2)/a.\tag{GF5}
\end{equation}
These formulas recover $a,b,c,e$. The first equation GF3 uniquely
determines $d$, and its second uniquely determines $f$ because
$a^3\ne0$. Thus GF5 is a full inverse on that open set.
At $a=0$, GF3 forces $bc=1$ and $-b^3c=1$, hence $b=\pm i$.
The original chart GF2 has $b=i$, $c=-i$, and contains all such
states: its inverse there is
\[
 y=id,\qquad z=(e+7iy^2)/2,\qquad
 w=(f-3iy^3+4yz)/i.
\]
It contains none of the $a=0,b=-i$ states. This description of the
chart is used explicitly in every fibre count below.

\section{Exact inverse over every target}
For $u=(u_0,u_2,u_3,u_4)$ define
\begin{equation}
 H_u(U,V)=u_0U^4+U^3V+u_2U^2V^2+u_3UV^3+u_4V^4,
 \qquad h_u(x)=H_u(x,1).\tag{GF6}
\end{equation}
The fixed $U^3V$ coefficient is part of the original map, not a
choice made during this calculation.

\begin{theorem}[Complete fibre, including every multiple-root case]
Every finite simple root $r$ of $h_u$ contributes exactly two
distinct points of $P^{-1}(u)$. Their complete coordinates are
\begin{align}
 a^2&=1/h_u'(r),\qquad a\in\{\text{both square roots}\},\nonumber\\
 y&=-r-i/a,\nonumber\\
 z&=u_0/a^3+2r/a+3i/a^2,\nonumber\\
 w&=7ir^2/a+(u_2-17r+u_0r^2)/a^2-13i/a^3-2u_0/a^4.
 \tag{GF7}
\end{align}
A finite multiple root contributes no point. When $u_0=0$ there
is exactly one additional point, namely
\begin{equation}
 q_\infty(u)=
 \left(0,u_2,\frac{i(7u_2^2-u_3)}2,
                  11u_2^3-2u_2u_3-u_4\right).\tag{GF8}
\end{equation}
There are no other points. In particular, writing $m(u)$ for the
number of distinct finite simple roots,
\begin{equation}
 \#P^{-1}(u)=2m(u)+\mathbf1_{\{u_0=0\}}.\tag{GF9}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A source point with $a\ne0$ gives the marked finite root
$r=-b/a$ of $H_u=LC$. The quotient factor is necessarily
\begin{align*}
 b&=-ar,&c&=u_0/a,&d&=(1+u_0r)/a,\\
 e&=(u_2+r+u_0r^2)/a,&
 f&=(u_3+u_2r+r^2+u_0r^3)/a.
\end{align*}
These formulas follow by comparing successive coefficients in
$H_u=a(U-rV)C$. The final constant coefficient is equivalent to
$h_u(r)=0$. Evaluating $C$ at $(-b,a)=(ar,a)$ gives
\[
 R(L,C)=a^3 C(r,1)=a^2 h_u'(r).
\]
Thus GF3 holds exactly when $a^2h_u'(r)=1$. A multiple root is
excluded by this equation, while each simple root supplies exactly
two nonzero values of $a$. The other incidence equation follows
without a further constraint:
$ad+bc=1+u_0r-u_0r=1$. The complete inverse GF5 now gives GF7;
for example substitution in $w=(e-2z+7iy^2)/a$ yields all four
displayed terms of its last line. These operations are reversible,
so they prove both sufficiency and exhaustiveness on $a\ne0$.
Distinct roots give different $r=-b/a$, and the two signs of $a$
give distinct source points.

If $a=0$ then $u_0=ac=0$. Conversely, GF1 on this hyperplane reads
\[
 P(0,y,z,w)=(0,y,2iz+7y^2,-4iyz-w-3y^3).
\]
Solving successively gives exactly GF8. In projective factor terms,
$u_0=0$ makes $[U:V]=[1:0]$ a simple root: $H_u=V(U^3+\cdots)$
and the leading coefficient of the parenthesis is exactly one.
Its two resultant-one factor scalings have $b=\pm i$, but GF2
retains only $b=i$. This is precisely the single point just solved,
regardless of any multiple finite roots. Hence there are no omitted
chart exceptions, and GF9 follows.
\end{proof}

For clarity the exhaustive multiplicity counts are
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
\text{target chart}&\text{finite root multiplicities}&\#P^{-1}(u)\\\hline
u_0\ne0&1+1+1+1&8\\
u_0\ne0&2+1+1&4\\
u_0\ne0&3+1&2\\
u_0\ne0&2+2\text{ or }4&0\\\hline
u_0=0&1+1+1&7\\
u_0=0&2+1&3\\
u_0=0&3&1
\end{array}
\]
Every row follows directly from GF9. The polynomial always has
degree four or three, because the cubic coefficient is fixed at one.

\section{A short complete Jacobian proof}
On $a\ne0$, use parameters $(a,r,A,B)$, where $A=u_0$ and $B=u_2$.
The two equations $h_u(r)=0$, $a^2h_u'(r)=1$ solve as
\begin{equation}
 u_3=a^{-2}-4Ar^3-3r^2-2Br,
 \qquad u_4=3Ar^4+2r^3+Br^2-r/a^2.\tag{GF10}
\end{equation}
GF7 gives the source parameterization
\[
 q(a,r,A,B)=\left(a,-r-i/a,A/a^3+2r/a+3i/a^2,
 7ir^2/a+(B-17r+Ar^2)/a^2-13i/a^3-2A/a^4\right).
\]
Its Jacobian determinant in this parameter order is $-a^{-5}$:
after its first row, the remaining triangular minor has diagonal
$-1,a^{-3},a^{-2}$. For the target parameterization
$(A,B,u_3,u_4)$, reorder the first two parameter columns past the
last two; this permutation has positive sign. The remaining
two-by-two determinant is
\[
 \det\begin{pmatrix}
 -2/a^3&-(12Ar^2+6r+2B)\\
 2r/a^3&r(12Ar^2+6r+2B)-a^{-2}
 \end{pmatrix}=2a^{-5}.
\]
The chain rule therefore proves
\begin{equation}
 \det DP=-2\quad\text{on }a\ne0.\tag{GF11}
\end{equation}
Every original source point with $a\ne0$ has these parameters by
GF5--7, so this identity holds on that entire open set. Both sides
of GF11 are polynomials in the original coordinates; their
difference vanishes on a nonempty dense open set and is consequently
the zero polynomial. This proves GF11 everywhere, including the
$a=0$ chart, with its original constant and sign.

\section{The exact omitted image}
The only quartic multiplicity patterns with no simple root are
$2+2$ and $4$. Equivalently a quartic with $u_0\ne0$ and no simple
root is the square of a quadratic times its leading coefficient.
Because its cubic coefficient is one, it must have the form
\[
 H_u(U,V)=u_0\left(U^2+\frac{1}{2u_0}UV+u_3V^2\right)^2.
\]
Indeed, write the monic quadratic as $U^2+pUV+qV^2$; comparison
of cubic and linear coefficients gives $p=1/(2u_0)$ and $q=u_3$.
The remaining coefficients then give the exact image complement
\begin{equation}
 \C^4\setminus P(\C^4)
 =\{4u_0u_2-1-8u_0^2u_3=0,\quad u_4-u_0u_3^2=0\}.
 \tag{GF12}
\end{equation}
The first equation excludes $u_0=0$. Conversely the two equations
give exactly the displayed square. GF9 therefore proves both
inclusions in GF12, not only exclusion of the square targets.

\section{Discriminant and the genuine eight-sheet domain}
Write $A=u_0,B=u_2,C=u_3,D=u_4$. Define the original binary-quartic
discriminant polynomial by
\begin{align}
 \mathfrak D(u)={}&256A^3D^3-128A^2B^2D^2+144A^2BC^2D
 -27A^2C^4-192A^2CD^2\nonumber\\
 &+16AB^4D-4AB^3C^2-80AB^2CD+18ABC^3
 +144ABD^2-6AC^2D\nonumber\\
 &-4B^3D+B^2C^2+18BCD-4C^3-27D^2.
 \tag{GF13}
\end{align}
For $A\ne0$, the Sylvester determinant of $h_u$ and $h_u'$,
divided by $A$, expands to GF13. If $r_1,\ldots,r_4$ are its
roots counted with multiplicity, that same determinant gives
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak D=A^6\prod_{j<k}(r_j-r_k)^2.\tag{GF14}
\end{equation}
One can verify this identity first for distinct roots: the
resultant is $A^3\prod_jh_u'(r_j)$, and
$\prod_jh_u'(r_j)=A^4\prod_{j<k}(r_j-r_k)^2$ because
$(-1)^{4\cdot3/2}=1$. Polynomial continuity gives the identity
also at multiple roots. At $A=0$, GF13 becomes
\[
 B^2C^2-4C^3-4B^3D+18BCD-27D^2,
\]
the discriminant of the monic cubic $x^3+Bx^2+Cx+D$. The same
resultant calculation in degree three proves that it vanishes
exactly at a repeated finite root. Since the projective root at
infinity is simple when $A=0$, GF13 vanishes exactly when $H_u$
has any multiple projective root, on both charts.

Let
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal B=\{u\in\C^4:u_0\mathfrak D(u)\ne0\},\qquad
 R=\C[u_0,u_2,u_3,u_4,(u_0\mathfrak D)^{-1}].\tag{GF15}
\end{equation}
Over this precise domain the map $P$ is an eight-sheet finite
unramified cover. Its complete coordinate algebra is
\begin{equation}
 S=R[r,a]/(h_u(r),\ a^2h_u'(r)-1).\tag{GF16}
\end{equation}
Here is a direct proof of each assertion. Since $u_0$ is a unit,
the first relation is a monic relation of degree four after division
by $u_0$, and $R[r]/(h_u)$ is free of rank four over $R$ with basis
$1,r,r^2,r^3$. The resultant of $h_u$ and $h_u'$ is a unit in $R$.
The adjugate of their Sylvester matrix therefore gives a Bezout
identity over $R$, so $h_u'(r)$ is a unit in this rank-four algebra.
The second relation is consequently the monic relation
$a^2=h_u'(r)^{-1}$. Thus $S$ is free of rank eight over $R$, with
basis $1,r,r^2,r^3,a,ar,ar^2,ar^3$.

The original chart inverse GF7 has entries in $S$: $a$ is a unit
with inverse $a h_u'(r)$. In the other direction, for an original
source point over $\mathcal B$ the identity $u_0=ac$ makes $a$
a unit, and $r=-b/a$ is regular. GF3--7 prove these two maps are
mutually inverse. Thus GF16 is the coordinate algebra of the
\emph{actual} preimage $P^{-1}(\mathcal B)$, rather than a new
eight-point model.

The derivative matrix of the two defining equations of GF16 with
respect to $(r,a)$ has determinant
$2a\,h_u'(r)^2$, a unit. This proves that each of its eight simple
solutions gives one local holomorphic inverse, equivalently that
the finite free map is unramified. Explicitly the four simple roots
vary holomorphically in a sufficiently small target neighbourhood,
and each nonzero $1/h_u'(r)$ has its two separate holomorphic
square roots there. GF7 gives eight disjoint local inverse charts.
There are exactly eight by GF9. These statements require neither
a chosen global square-root branch nor identification of its two signs.

\section{The complete nonproper-value locus}
A target $u$ is called a nonproper value here if there is a sequence
$q_n\in\C^4$ escaping every compact set with $P(q_n)\to u$.
For this polynomial map that is the local failure of properness
near $u$. The exact locus is
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad\mathcal N(P)=\{u_0=0\}\ \cup\ \{\mathfrak D(u)=0\}.\quad}
 \tag{GF17}
\end{equation}

First take $u\in\mathcal B$ and a convergent sequence of targets
$u_n\to u$. On a sufficiently small closed neighbourhood of $u$,
$u_0$ and $\mathfrak D$ are bounded away from zero. Every finite
root is bounded: after dividing $h$ by $u_0$, any root has modulus
at most one plus the maximum modulus of its nonleading
coefficients. This follows by comparing the leading term with
the finite geometric sum of the remaining terms outside that disk.
Moreover $|h_{u_n}'(r_n)|$ is bounded away from zero uniformly over
all roots. Otherwise a convergent subsequence of the bounded
$r_n$ would give a root $r$ of $h_u$ with $h_u'(r)=0$,
contradicting $\mathfrak D(u)\ne0$. The derivatives also have a
uniform upper bound, by the root and coefficient bounds. Hence
$a^2=1/h'(r)$ bounds both $a$ and $a^{-1}$; GF7 then bounds every
original source coordinate. There is no $a=0$ solution on this
neighbourhood. Thus no sequence above $u_n$ can escape, proving
$\mathcal N(P)\subseteq\C^4\setminus\mathcal B$.

For the reverse inclusion at $u_0=0$, let GF8 be
$q_0=(0,y_0,z_0,w_0)$ and set
$q_\epsilon=(\epsilon,y_0,z_0,w_0)$ for real $\epsilon>0$.
On $a\ne0$ the exact factor-sign involution is
\begin{equation}
 \Sigma(a,y,z,w)=\left(-a,y+\frac{2i}{a},\frac{6i}{a^2}-z,
 w-\frac{14iy^2}{a}+\frac{28y}{a^2}+\frac{40i}{a^3}\right).
 \tag{GF18}
\end{equation}
To verify its invariance, substitute GF18 in GF2: $a,b,c,e$
all change sign. GF3 then forces $d$ and $f$ also to change sign
because $a\ne0$. Both factors in GF4 change sign, so
$P\Sigma=P$. The full inverse GF5 proves $\Sigma^2=I$ as well.
Now the second coordinate of $\Sigma q_\epsilon$ is
$y_0+2i/\epsilon$ and escapes, while
$P\Sigma q_\epsilon=Pq_\epsilon\to Pq_0=u$.
Every point of the entire hyperplane $u_0=0$ is consequently
nonproper, including its discriminant intersections.

Finally let $u_0\ne0$ and $\mathfrak D(u)=0$. Choose any multiple
root $r_0$ of $h_u$ and define
\[
 h_\epsilon(x)=h_u(x)+\epsilon(x-r_0),\qquad
 u_\epsilon=(u_0,u_2,u_3+\epsilon,u_4-\epsilon r_0).
\]
These preserve the original fixed cubic coefficient.
The root $r_0$ persists and has derivative exactly $\epsilon$.
For real $\epsilon>0$ choose $a=\epsilon^{-1/2}$ and apply the
complete inverse GF7 to this simple root. It gives a genuine
source point $q_\epsilon$ with $Pq_\epsilon=u_\epsilon\to u$,
while its first coordinate tends to infinity. This argument works
for every multiplicity of $r_0$ and does not require the other roots
to be simple. It proves the remaining inclusion in GF17.

\section{The literal arithmetic quartet as a marked target quartic}
Retain the original four distinct roots, with both original signs:
\[
 \rho_{\varepsilon,\eta}=\tfrac12+\varepsilon\delta+i\eta\gamma,
 \qquad\varepsilon,\eta\in\{1,-1\},\quad
 0<\delta<\tfrac12,\quad\gamma>2.
\]
Their sum is exactly two. Therefore the quartic with these literal
roots and the required original cubic coefficient one is
\begin{equation}
 H_\rho(U,V)=-\tfrac12\prod_{\varepsilon,\eta}
                    (U-\rho_{\varepsilon,\eta}V).\tag{GF19}
\end{equation}
The factor $-1/2$ is forced by the cubic coefficient and is retained
in every derivative, discriminant, and inverse below. With
$x=S-1/2$, its affine polynomial is
\[
 h_\rho(S)=-\tfrac12\bigl(x^4+2(\gamma^2-\delta^2)x^2
                              +(\delta^2+\gamma^2)^2\bigr).
\]
Expanding gives the exact target
\begin{equation}
 u_\rho(\delta)=\left(-\tfrac12,\
 \delta^2-\gamma^2-\tfrac34,\
 \gamma^2-\delta^2+\tfrac14,\
 -\tfrac1{32}-\frac{\gamma^2-\delta^2}{4}
                     -\frac{(\delta^2+\gamma^2)^2}{2}\right).
 \tag{GF20}
\end{equation}
At the marked root $\rho_{\varepsilon,\eta}$,
\begin{equation}
 h_\rho'(\rho_{\varepsilon,\eta})
       =4\delta\gamma(\varepsilon\gamma-i\eta\delta),\qquad
 a_{\varepsilon,\eta,\pm}^{2}
       =\frac1{4\delta\gamma(\varepsilon\gamma-i\eta\delta)}.
 \tag{GF21}
\end{equation}
To verify the derivative, the three differences from that root are
$2i\eta\gamma$, $2\varepsilon\delta$, and
$2\varepsilon\delta+2i\eta\gamma$; multiply them and the retained
leading coefficient $-1/2$. This gives precisely GF21. Both square
roots are present at every one of the four marked roots.

In the order $(+,+),(+,-),(-,+),(-,-)$ the original Vandermonde is
$\Delta_\rho=-64\delta^2\gamma^2(\delta^2+\gamma^2)$. Hence
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak D(u_\rho)=\Delta_\rho^2/64
                   =64\delta^4\gamma^4(\delta^2+\gamma^2)^2>0.
 \tag{GF22}
\end{equation}
The denominator $64$ is the sixth power of the retained factor
$2$. Thus $u_\rho\in\mathcal B$, and GF7 and GF21 give all
eight original chart states over it. Unlike the earlier target
$UV(U-V)(U+V)$, it has no root at infinity and loses no sign
partner from the source chart.

For fixed $\gamma$ and $\delta\downarrow0$, GF19 tends to
\begin{equation}
 H_0=-\tfrac12\left((U-V/2)^2+\gamma^2V^2\right)^2.\tag{GF23}
\end{equation}
This target is on the omitted square surface GF12, so its complete
source fibre is empty. The eight nearby states all escape; they
do not become fewer finite chart points. Indeed GF21 gives
\[
 |a|=\bigl(4\delta\gamma\sqrt{\gamma^2+\delta^2}\bigr)^{-1/2},
 \qquad \sqrt\delta\,|a|\longrightarrow\frac1{2\gamma}.
\]
For each continuous choice of its sign branch,
\begin{equation}
 \sqrt\delta\,a_{\varepsilon,\eta,\pm}\longrightarrow
 \kappa_{\varepsilon,\pm},\qquad
 \kappa_{\varepsilon,\pm}^2=\frac1{4\varepsilon\gamma^2}.
 \tag{GF24}
\end{equation}
GF7 then gives
$y\to-(1/2+i\eta\gamma)$ and $z,w\to0$. Consequently, in
the original source coordinates,
\begin{equation}
 \sqrt\delta\,q_{\varepsilon,\eta,\pm}(\delta)
       \longrightarrow\kappa_{\varepsilon,\pm}e_a,
 \qquad e_a=(1,0,0,0)^{\mathsf T}.\tag{GF25}
\end{equation}
Each claim follows from the displayed inverse formulas; no moving
period or limiting identification of distinct root coordinates is
used to infer these limits.

\section{All eight escaping branches in one fixed original conductor norm}
Fix once and for all an admitted reference quartet with parameter
$\delta_*>0$, its original conductor, an original permitted source
order $s$, and its original target Gamma norm on $W=\cP_3$.
Let $\Psi_*:\C^4\to W$ be the exact invertible receiving map
constructed from that reference conductor in FC26--35
above. Its entries include the reference period and every
reference moment; none is varied in this section. Define
\[
 K=\begin{pmatrix}
 0&i&1/\sqrt2&1/\sqrt2\\
 0&-1&-1-\sqrt2 i&1-\sqrt2 i\\
 i/2&-3i&2\sqrt2+6i&-2\sqrt2+6i\\
 0&13&-34-19\sqrt2 i&34-19\sqrt2 i
 \end{pmatrix},\quad \det K=77\sqrt2 i/2.
\]
For complete coordinates, let $V_*=(\rho_{*,j}^n)_{0\le n\le3,1\le j\le4}$
be the reference root Vandermonde, and let $\mu_j$ and $v$ be
the original reference conductor moments and their actual first
nonzero order. In the target basis $(1,S',S'^2,S'^3)$ the complete
matrix of this fixed map is
\[
 [\Psi_*]=B_{v,*}(V_*^{\mathsf T})^{-1}K^{-1},\qquad
 (B_{v,*})_{kj}=\begin{cases}
 \binom{v+j}{k}\mu_{v+j-k},&0\le k\le j\le3,\\
 0,&k>j.
 \end{cases}
\]
The first determinant is
$\det B_{v,*}=\mu_v^4\prod_{j=0}^3\binom{v+j}{v}\ne0$;
the reference Vandermonde is nonzero and $K$ has the determinant
displayed above. This proves the asserted invertibility with all
original factors retained. The target norm in this monomial frame
is the original one, not the Euclidean norm of the column:
\[
 \|p\|_{\Gamma,*}^2=
 \int_{\mathbb R}|p(c_*+iy)|^2
 \frac{(2\pi)^{s/2}2^{s/2}}{4\pi\Gamma(s/2)}
 |\Gamma(s/4+iy/2)|^2\,dy,\qquad
 c_*=a_{\mathrm{amp},*}/2-4,
\]
where $s\in\{1,a_{\mathrm{amp},*}\}$ is the fixed original
reference source order. This integral defines a positive definite
norm on $\cP_3$: its density is positive on the real axis and a
nonzero polynomial cannot vanish on that entire axis. Finiteness is
the original WCF2 orthogonal-polynomial norm identity
$h_{n,s}=(2\pi)^{s/2}n!(s/2)_n$, applied to the finite expansion
of the polynomial in that frame, with no change of measure.
With these exact maps and norms, define
\[
 P_{W,*}=\Psi_*P\Psi_*^{-1},\qquad
 p_\delta=\Psi_*u_\rho(\delta),\qquad
 Z_{\varepsilon,\eta,\pm}(\delta)
              =\Psi_*q_{\varepsilon,\eta,\pm}(\delta).
\]
The variable $\delta$ in these formulas parametrizes an auxiliary
target path inside this \emph{fixed} operator and its \emph{fixed}
norm. It does not move the reference arithmetic period. Exact
conjugacy gives all eight fibre identities
\[
 P_{W,*}(Z_{\varepsilon,\eta,\pm}(\delta))=p_\delta.
\]
GF25 and continuity of the fixed linear map give the actual norm
asymptote, for every one of the eight branches,
\begin{equation}
 \sqrt\delta\,\|Z_{\varepsilon,\eta,\pm}(\delta)\|_{\Gamma,*}
       \longrightarrow\frac{\|\Psi_*e_a\|_{\Gamma,*}}{2\gamma}>0.
 \tag{GF26}
\end{equation}
The strict inequality follows because $\Psi_*$ is injective and
the original Gamma norm is positive definite.

The target displacement in its original coordinates is exactly
\begin{equation}
 u_\rho(\delta)-u_\rho(0)
 =\delta^2 d_\gamma-\tfrac12\delta^4e_w,
 \quad d_\gamma=(0,1,-1,\tfrac14-\gamma^2)^{\mathsf T},
 \quad e_w=(0,0,0,1)^{\mathsf T}.\tag{GF27}
\end{equation}
Subtract GF20 at zero to verify every coefficient. Therefore
$\delta^{-2}\|p_\delta-p_0\|_{\Gamma,*}
\to\|\Psi_*d_\gamma\|_{\Gamma,*}>0$.
Combining this with GF26 proves the complete original-norm rate
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \|p_\delta-p_0\|_{\Gamma,*}^{1/4}
 \|Z_{\varepsilon,\eta,\pm}(\delta)\|_{\Gamma,*}
 \longrightarrow
 \frac{\|\Psi_*d_\gamma\|_{\Gamma,*}^{1/4}
       \|\Psi_*e_a\|_{\Gamma,*}}{2\gamma}>0.
 \quad}\tag{GF28}
\end{equation}
The limiting point $p_0$ has no finite preimage under $P_{W,*}$,
because GF12 and the exact conjugacy transfer that assertion as
well. For completeness, the entire nonproper-value locus of this
fixed nonlinear receiving map is precisely
$\Psi_*(\{u_0\mathfrak D(u)=0\})$: invertible linear maps preserve
boundedness, escaping sequences, and convergence. This statement
concerns $P_{W,*}$; the original linear conductor remains the
invertible intertwiner defining $\Psi_*$.

GF26--28 avoid using a singular limit of a moving Vandermonde or a
moving conductor norm. Such a limit would be a separate calculation:
the construction of $\Psi_\delta$ itself assumes distinct original
roots, and its original Vandermonde vanishes at $\delta=0$.
Everything asserted here has instead been proved with the reference
operator, its full original coefficients, and its original norm held
fixed. No assertion about RH follows from introducing this nonlinear
map or this auxiliary target path.
