       &(t^5/\kappa)J^{-1}\longrightarrow\tfrac12e_1\ell_0\\
 2&t^5\wedge^2J\longrightarrow(v_0\wedge w_0)e_{34}^*
       &t^5\wedge^2J^{-1}\longrightarrow
                    -\tfrac12e_{12}(\ell'_0\wedge\ell_0)\\
 3&(t^5/\kappa)\wedge^3J\longrightarrow b_3e_{234}^*
       &t^3\wedge^3J^{-1}\longrightarrow e_{124}\eta_3\\
 4&\wedge^4J=-2&\wedge^4J^{-1}=-\tfrac12
\end{array}.}\tag{FD14}
\]
Every entry and sign can also be read as the following
explicit wedge coefficient rows:
\[
\begin{aligned}
 v_0\wedge w_0&=(1,-2r_0,r_0^2,4r_0^3,-3r_0^4,2r_0^5)^T,\\
 \ell'_0\wedge\ell_0&=(2r_0^5,3r_0^4,4r_0^3,r_0^2,2r_0,1).
\end{aligned}\tag{FD15}
\]
For \(r=1\) on the forward side, FD7 gives the limit
entry by entry. For \(r=2\), the only order \(t^{-5}\)
minor uses columns \(z,w\). The potential order \(t^{-5}\)
term from columns \(y,z\) vanishes because both leading
columns are multiples of \(v(r)\); all remaining terms
have smaller pole. This proves the full second-compound
limit, not only one of its entries.

For the inverse first-compound limit, its first row in
FD4--5 is exactly
\[
 \frac{\kappa}{2t^5}\ell_r-\frac1{2t^3}\ell'_r.
 \tag{FD16}
\]
Multiplication by \(t^5/\kappa\), followed by FD13,
gives \(\ell_0/2\). The other rows tend to zero under
this factor: their terms contain positive powers of \(t\),
or such powers times \(t^2/\kappa\), after the multiplication.
For example the \(y\)-row becomes
\(t^3\ell_r/\kappa-it^4\ell'_r/(2\kappa)
+i t^2\ell_r/2\), which tends to zero. The \(z,w\)
rows follow directly from their bounded coefficient
polynomials in FD5 and the same relation
\(t^2/\kappa=O(u^2)\). This proves the inverse limit
also when \(\kappa\to0\), as happens for \(m=3,4\).

All remaining limits follow from the exact complementary
minor identity, with its original determinant:
\[
 \det(J^{-1})_{I,J}
 =(-1)^{\sum I+\sum J}\frac{\det J_{J^c,I^c}}{\det J}
 =-\tfrac12(-1)^{\sum I+\sum J}\det J_{J^c,I^c}.
 \tag{FD17}
\]
This identity follows from the adjugate formula, or by
expanding the determinant on complementary coordinate
subsets. For the third forward compound use the same
identity for \(J^{-1}\) and the first inverse limit.
It gives the column \((1,-r_0,r_0^2,-r_0^4)^T\)
at column \(234\). For the second inverse compound
it gives only row \(12\), with the row in FD15 multiplied
by \(-1/2\). For the third inverse compound it gives
only row \(124\), equal to \(\eta_3\). Thus the signs
and coordinate complements in FD14 are fully determined.

One can state these limits directly in the complex Puiseux
parameter without hiding any phase. For a fixed \(j,\pm\)
write \(\theta=\theta_{j,\pm}\), \(K=K_j\). The forward
scalings and coefficients are
\[
\begin{aligned}
 u^{3(m-1)}J&\longrightarrow\theta^{-3}v_0e_3^*,\\
 u^{5(m-1)}\wedge^2J&\longrightarrow
                     \theta^{-5}(v_0\wedge w_0)e_{34}^*,\\
 u^{3m-1}\wedge^3J&\longrightarrow K\theta^{-5}b_3e_{234}^*,
\end{aligned}\tag{FD18}
\]
and the inverse limits are respectively
\[
\begin{aligned}
 u^{3m-1}J^{-1}&\longrightarrow
                         \frac{K}{2\theta^5}e_1\ell_0,\\
 u^{5(m-1)}\wedge^2J^{-1}&\longrightarrow
                         -\frac1{2\theta^5}e_{12}
                                      (\ell'_0\wedge\ell_0),\\
 u^{3(m-1)}\wedge^3J^{-1}&\longrightarrow
                         \theta^{-3}e_{124}\eta_3.
\end{aligned}\tag{FD19}
\]
These give the phase for every one of the \(2m\) signed
branches, including the effect of \(t\mapsto-t\).

\section{The original metric and all constants}
Fix the original receiving map
\[
 \Psi=B_v(V_\rho^{\mathsf T})^{-1}K_{\rm ES}^{-1},\qquad
 (B_v)_{kj}=\begin{cases}
 \binom{v+j}{k}\mu_{v+j-k},&k\le j,\\0,&k>j,
 \end{cases}\quad 0\le k,j\le3,
 \tag{FD20}
\]
with its original fixed conductor order, moments,
quartet Vandermonde and collision matrix. Its determinant
is nonzero by the already proved FC27 and the original
\(\mu_v\ne0\). The norm on the target polynomial space
is unchanged:
\[
 \|p\|_\Gamma^2=\int_\R|p(c_*+iy)|^2dm_s(y),\qquad
 dm_s(y)=\frac{(2\pi)^{s/2}2^{s/2}}{4\pi\Gamma(s/2)}
                |\Gamma(s/4+iy/2)|^2dy .
 \tag{FD21}
\]
Let \(G=\Psi^*\mathcal H_\Gamma\Psi\) be its full positive
pullback. In the original monomial frame every entry is
explicit:
\[
 (\mathcal H_\Gamma)_{jk}
 =\sum_{p=0}^j\sum_{q=0}^k
 \binom jp\binom kq c_*^{j+k-p-q}(-i)^pi^q m_{p+q},
 \tag{FD22}
\]
where \(b=s/2\), \(M=(2\pi)^{s/2}\),
\[
 m_0=M,\quad m_2=Mb,\quad m_4=M(3b^2+2b),\quad
 m_6=M(15b^3+30b^2+16b),\qquad m_1=m_3=m_5=0.
\]
These are the original WCF Gamma moments \cite{WCF}.
All period coefficients, phases, center, mass and cross
terms remain in FD20--22. The fixed \(\Psi\) conjugates
the polynomial \(P\) to \(P_W=\Psi P\Psi^{-1}\); therefore
the derivative \(J\) uses the same \(G\) on its domain
and codomain. No moving-period limit is taken.

Define the three strictly positive original constants
\[
\begin{aligned}
 C_1&=\sqrt{v_0^*Gv_0}\sqrt{(G^{-1})_{33}},\\
 C_2&=\sqrt{\det([v_0\ w_0]^*G[v_0\ w_0])}
                       \sqrt{\det(G^{-1})_{34,34}},\\
 C_3&=\sqrt{G_{11}}\sqrt{\ell_0G^{-1}\ell_0^*}.
\end{aligned}\tag{FD23}
\]
Positivity follows from \(v_{0,1}=1\), independence
of \(v_0,w_0\), \(\ell_{0,4}=1\), and positive definiteness
of \(G\). The norm of a rank-one exterior map
\(x\lambda\) is \(\|x\|\|\lambda\|\): Cauchy--Schwarz
gives the upper bound and the Riesz representative of
\(\lambda\) attains it. The Gram determinant formula
for exterior norms therefore gives \(C_1,C_2\) for the
first two forward limits and \(C_3/2\) for the first
inverse limit in FD14.

For an invertible endomorphism of this fixed four-dimensional
Hermitian space, products of singular values prove the exact
identity
\[
 \|\wedge^rJ^{-1}\|_G
            =\frac{\|\wedge^{4-r}J\|_G}{|\det J|}
            =\tfrac12\|\wedge^{4-r}J\|_G .
 \tag{FD24}
\]
Indeed the inverse singular values are the reciprocals
in reverse order; canceling their product proves the
formula. Consequently the complete exterior asymptotes
in the original norm are
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
 r&\|\wedge^rJ\|_G&\|\wedge^rJ^{-1}\|_G\\\hline
 1&\sim C_1|t|^{-3}&\sim C_3|\kappa|/(2|t|^5)\\
 2&\sim C_2|t|^{-5}&\sim C_2/(2|t|^5)\\
 3&\sim C_3|\kappa|/|t|^5&\sim C_1/(2|t|^3)\\
 4&2&1/2
\end{array}.\tag{FD25}
\]
The limits are norm limits of the full finite matrices,
not estimates from sampled numerical singular values.

Successive ratios of forward exterior norms give every
singular value in decreasing order:
\[
\boxed{\quad
 \sigma_1\sim C_1|t|^{-3},\qquad
 \sigma_2\sim\frac{C_2}{C_1}|t|^{-2},\qquad
 \sigma_3\sim\frac{C_3}{C_2}|\kappa|,\qquad
 \sigma_4\sim\frac{2|t|^5}{C_3|\kappa|}.
 \quad}\tag{FD26}
\]
For completeness \(\|\wedge^rJ\|\) is the product of the
largest \(r\) singular values: diagonalizing \(J^*J\) in
the fixed metric and taking wedges gives all \(r\)-fold
products. This proves the ratio step and verifies EA36.

The exact coefficient \(c_m\) gives
\[
 |t|\sim\sqrt m\,|c_m|^{1/(2m)}
                       |\varepsilon|^{(m-1)/(2m)},\qquad
 |\kappa|\sim m(m-1)|c_m|^{2/m}
                       |\varepsilon|^{(m-2)/m}.
 \tag{FD27}
\]
Substituting, with no amplitude set to one, yields
\[
\boxed{\begin{aligned}
 \sigma_1&\sim\frac{C_1}{m^{3/2}|c_m|^{3/(2m)}}
                  |\varepsilon|^{-3(m-1)/(2m)},\\
 \sigma_2&\sim\frac{C_2}{mC_1|c_m|^{1/m}}
                  |\varepsilon|^{-(m-1)/m},\\
 \sigma_3&\sim\frac{m(m-1)C_3|c_m|^{2/m}}{C_2}
                  |\varepsilon|^{(m-2)/m},\\
 \sigma_4&\sim\frac{2m^{3/2}|c_m|^{1/(2m)}}{(m-1)C_3}
                  |\varepsilon|^{(3m-1)/(2m)}.
\end{aligned}}\tag{FD28}
\]
All four constants multiply to exactly \(2\), agreeing
with FD8, and every phase has already been recorded in
the stronger complex matrix limits FD18--19. The powers are
\[
\begin{array}{c|rrrr}
m&\sigma_1&\sigma_2&\sigma_3&\sigma_4\\\hline
2&-3/4&-1/2&0&5/4\\
3&-1&-2/3&1/3&4/3\\
4&-9/8&-3/4&1/2&11/8
\end{array}.
\]
They belong to the specified constant-term path FD10.

\section{Original singular flags and exact fixed-plane volumes}
Every adjacent singular-value ratio tends to infinity
by FD28. Thus the leading singular flags are unique for
sufficiently small nonzero parameter. The leading right
one-, two-, and three-planes converge, in the original
metric, to
\[
 G^{-1}\Span(e_3),\qquad
 G^{-1}\Span(e_3,e_4),\qquad
 G^{-1}\Span(e_2,e_3,e_4).
 \tag{FD29}
\]
The corresponding leading left planes converge to
\[
 \Span(v_0),\qquad \Span(v_0,w_0),\qquad \ker\ell_0.
 \tag{FD30}
\]
For the proof, divide each full forward compound by its
norm and apply FD14. Its limit is a unit-norm rank-one
map, so its products with its adjoint tend to rank-one
orthogonal projections. The top eigenspace projections
converge: any subsequential limit of a unit top eigenvector
satisfies the limiting eigenvector equation and lies in
the unique limiting range. The right Riesz representative
of the coordinate wedge functional \(e_J^*\) is
\(\bigwedge_{j\in J}G^{-1}e_j\). The left third compound
vector \(b_3\) is decomposable and represents \(\ker\ell_0\),
as follows by taking its wedge coefficients or the
complementary-minor formula FD17. This proves both flags.
In particular the smallest right singular line tends to
\(\C e_1\), and the smallest left line to
\(\C G^{-1}\ell_0^*\), by taking their original-metric
orthogonal complements. Their actual polynomial-space
images are obtained by the fixed \(\Psi\).

The exact volume distortion on the fixed coordinate
input flags \(\Span(e_3)\), \(\Span(e_3,e_4)\), and
\(\Span(e_2,e_3,e_4)\) is respectively
\[
\begin{aligned}
 &\sim |t|^{-6}\frac{v_0^*Gv_0}{G_{33}},\\
 &\sim |t|^{-10}
              \frac{\det([v_0\ w_0]^*G[v_0\ w_0])}{\det G_{34,34}},\\
 &\sim |\kappa|^2|t|^{-10}
              \frac{\ell_0G^{-1}\ell_0^*}{(G^{-1})_{11}}.
\end{aligned}\tag{FD31}
\]
Each is a squared output Gram determinant divided by
the squared input volume in the original metric. For
the first two apply the compound limit to the displayed
coordinate wedge. For the third, the complementary
Gram identity gives
\(\|b_3\|_{\wedge^3G}^2
=\det G\,\ell_0G^{-1}\ell_0^*\) and
\(\det G_{234,234}=\det G(G^{-1})_{11}\).
This proves the last constant including its cross terms.
In degree four the squared-volume ratio is exactly four
at every finite point.

\section{Escape and the exact conductor relation}
Formula FD2 and the actual branches FD12 imply that
\(a=1/t\) is the sole divergent coordinate; the other
three tend to \((-r_0,0,0)\). Thus
\[
 |\varepsilon|^{(m-1)/(2m)}\|\Psi q\|_\Gamma
 \longrightarrow\frac{\sqrt{G_{11}}}
                         {\sqrt m\,|c_m|^{1/(2m)}}>0.
 \tag{FD32}
\]
The original factor is \(L=a(U-rV)\), so its actual
coefficient \(a\) diverges as well. This is intrinsic
factor-scale escape at the selected finite repeated root;
adding a missing \(a=0\) chart cannot supply a finite
limit of that coordinate. The image displacement is
exactly \(-\varepsilon\Psi e_4\). Consequently, if
\(\alpha_m=(m-1)/(2m)\),
\[
 \|\Psi(u_\varepsilon-u_0)\|_\Gamma^{\alpha_m}
 \|\Psi q\|_\Gamma
 \longrightarrow
 \frac{G_{44}^{\alpha_m/2}\sqrt{G_{11}}}
                         {\sqrt m\,|c_m|^{1/(2m)}}.
 \tag{FD33}
\]

The fixed original source map \(\Phi\) and conductor
restriction \(L=T_A|_U\) obey \(L\Phi=\Psi\).
For \(P_U=\Phi P\Phi^{-1}\), \(P_W=\Psi P\Psi^{-1}\),
differentiation at the matching points gives exactly
\[
 L\,DP_U(\Phi q)=DP_W(\Psi q)L.
 \tag{FD34}
\]
Both maps use their original respective norms; the
source-side formulas follow by replacing \(G\) by
the actual fixed \(G_U=\Phi^*\mathcal H_U\Phi\), with
no change to the differential matrices or phases.
In particular, exterior inverse transport is the exact
identity
\[
 \wedge^r DP_W^{-1}
   =(\wedge^rL)(\wedge^rDP_U^{-1})(\wedge^rL^{-1}).
 \tag{FD35}
\]
This is the original four-plane inverse from FC24, not
a variable conductor matrix or a claim that its leading
moment vanished. The finite-root degenerations above
are completely located in the specified nonlinear
derivative along its actual escaping branches.

\section{Exact verification and source use}
The independent script
\texttt{verify\_finite\_root\_native\_differential.py}
starts from FD1, differentiates it before substitution,
checks \(Pq=\pi\), both intermediate determinants,
\(JDq=D\pi\), both inverse products for FD4--5,
and every forward and inverse compound over
\(\mathbb Q(i)(t,r,A,B)\). Its full matrices and checks
are recorded in
\texttt{finite\_root\_native\_differential\_exact.json}.
No sampled period, artificial Gram or numerical
singular-value diagnostic is used in any proof above.
The Puiseux phases and the passage to the original metric
are proved explicitly in FD9--33.

\section{Residue duality on the complete signed-root algebra}
The finite completion FS1--25 retains nilpotents at a multiple
root. Here a nilpotent means a nonzero algebra element whose
positive power is zero. We compute a bilinear pairing that
detects every such element, the exact map from it to ordinary
trace, and both maps in the original conductor spaces.
The general relation between a coefficient-residue functional,
its Hankel matrix and the inverse Bezout matrix is classical;
we use Jouve and Rodriguez-Villegas \cite[Section 2, especially
Lemma \texttt{Q-lemma} and Theorem \texttt{thBsharp}]{JouveRV}.
All matrices and constants for the original quartic, its signed
extension and its conductor return are proved below.

Let $u=(A,B,C,D)$, with $A\ne0$, and retain
\begin{equation}
 h=Ar^4+r^3+Br^2+Cr+D,\quad
 E_u=\C[r]/(h),\quad
 S_u=E_u[t]/(t^2-h'),\quad
 \mathbf e=(1,r,r^2,r^3,t,tr,tr^2,tr^3).
 \tag{RD1}
\end{equation}
Division by $h$ has a unique remainder of degree at most three
because its leading coefficient is a unit. Thus $E_u$ has the
first four displayed basis vectors, and division by the monic
polynomial in $t$ proves that every element of $S_u$ has a
unique expression $R+tS$, with $R,S\in E_u$. Define
\begin{equation}
 \lambda_u(F)=\sum_{h(r_0)=0}\operatorname{Res}_{r=r_0}
                 \frac{F(r)\,dr}{h(r)}
     =\frac{[r^3](F\bmod h)}{A},\qquad
 \Lambda_u(R+tS)=\lambda_u(S),\qquad
 \mathcal B_u(F,G)=\Lambda_u(FG).
 \tag{RD2}
\end{equation}
The residue formula includes every multiple root. Subtracting
the remainder adds a polynomial differential with no finite
residue. For the remainder, its coefficient of $r^{-1}$ at
infinity is its cubic coefficient divided by $A$; the sum
of the finite residues is that coefficient. This proves RD2
directly and also proves that the definition descends to $E_u$.
The functional is complex-linear; the pairing is complex
bilinear and symmetric. No conjugation is used in RD2.

In the displayed order the two full Gram matrices and inverse are
\begin{align}
 H_u&=\begin{pmatrix}
 0&0&0&A^{-1}\\
 0&0&A^{-1}&-A^{-2}\\
 0&A^{-1}&-A^{-2}&A^{-3}-BA^{-2}\\
 A^{-1}&-A^{-2}&A^{-3}-BA^{-2}&-A^{-4}+2BA^{-3}-CA^{-2}
 \end{pmatrix},\nonumber\\
 J_u&=\begin{pmatrix}C&B&1&A\\B&1&A&0\\1&A&0&0\\A&0&0&0\end{pmatrix},
 &\mathcal B_u&\longleftrightarrow
 B_u=\begin{pmatrix}0&H_u\\H_u&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 B_u^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&J_u\\J_u&0\end{pmatrix}.
 \tag{RD3}
\end{align}
To prove every entry, put $l_j=\lambda_u(r^j)$. RD2 gives
$l_0=l_1=l_2=0$, $l_3=A^{-1}$; multiplying $h=0$ by
$1,r,r^2$ gives successively
$l_4=-A^{-2}$, $l_5=A^{-3}-BA^{-2}$ and
$l_6=-A^{-4}+2BA^{-3}-CA^{-2}$.
Consequently $(H_u)_{ij}=l_{i+j}$ for $0\le i,j\le3$.
The product $(R+tS)(P+tQ)$ has odd coefficient $RQ+SP$,
which proves the block matrix. Direct row multiplication
gives $H_uJ_u=J_uH_u=I_4$. Equivalently, $J_u$ is the
coefficient matrix of $(h(x)-h(y))/(x-y)$; in the sign
convention of \cite[Section 2.3]{JouveRV} it is $B(h,1)$.
Expansion along the reversed diagonal gives
\begin{equation}
 \det H_u=A^{-4},\qquad \det B_u=A^{-8}.
 \tag{RD4}
\end{equation}
In particular $B_u$ is invertible on the entire $A\ne0$
chart, including its discriminant. Explicit dual vectors
are $t\sum_j(J_u)_{ji}r^j$ for $r^i$, and
$\sum_j(J_u)_{ji}r^j$ for $tr^i$. Pairing against those
eight vectors recovers the eight original coefficients.

\section{The exact passage from residue to trace}
For $F\in S_u$ let $M_F$ denote multiplication by $F$.
Define $L=M_{h'}$ on $E_u$. Multiplication by $t$ and by
$2t^3$ in the order RD1 are
\begin{equation}
 M_t=\begin{pmatrix}0&L\\I_4&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 M_{2t^3}=2\begin{pmatrix}0&L^2\\L&0\end{pmatrix}.
 \tag{RD5}
\end{equation}
Every trace, including on a nonreduced fibre, satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{Tr}_{S_u}(M_F)=\Lambda_u(2t^3F),\qquad
 T_u:=\bigl(\operatorname{Tr}M_{e_i e_j}\bigr)_{ij}
    =B_uM_{2t^3}
    =2\begin{pmatrix}H_uL&0\\0&H_uL^2\end{pmatrix}.
 \tag{RD6}
\end{equation}
Here is a proof valid on each fibre without a distinct-root
assumption. On the length-$m$ algebra at $r_0$, multiplication
by $R$ is triangular in powers of $r-r_0$, and its diagonal
is $R(r_0)$ repeated $m$ times. Its trace is $mR(r_0)$.
Writing $h=(r-r_0)^m g$ shows that the residue of $h'R/h$
is also $mR(r_0)$. Addition over the roots gives
$\operatorname{Tr}_{E_u}M_R=\lambda_u(h'R)$.
On $E_u\oplus tE_u$ the diagonal blocks of $M_{R+tS}$
are both $M_R$, so its trace is $2\lambda_u(h'R)$.
The odd coefficient of $2t^3(R+tS)$ is $2h'R$.
This proves the functional identity and then the Gram identity.

The matrix of $M_r$ on $E_u$ is completely explicit:
\begin{equation}
 Z=\begin{pmatrix}
 0&0&0&-D/A\\1&0&0&-C/A\\0&1&0&-B/A\\0&0&1&-1/A
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad
 L=4AZ^3+3Z^2+2BZ+CI_4 .
 \tag{RD7}
\end{equation}
Thus RD5--6 contain no unevaluated quotient multiplication.
With the usual quartic discriminant retaining the factor $A^6$,
$\det L=\prod h'(r_j)=\operatorname{Disc}(h)/A^2$:
at four distinct roots each unordered difference contributes
its square and the six sign changes have positive product.
Both sides are polynomials in the coefficients with powers of
$A$ inverted, so this identity holds at repeated roots too.
Taking determinants in RD5--6 now proves
\begin{equation}
 \det M_{2t^3}=2^8(\det L)^3,\qquad
 \det T_u=\frac{256\operatorname{Disc}(h)^3}{A^{14}}.
 \tag{RD8}
\end{equation}
The determinant loss is exactly multiplication by $2t^3$.
The residue identification $S_u\to S_u^*$ in RD3 is an
isomorphism; its composition with $M_{2t^3}$ is the trace
identification. This specifies the actual morphism and its
kernel, rather than merely comparing the names of two forms.

\section{Every local nilpotent and every lost trace direction}
At a root of multiplicity $m\in\{2,3,4\}$ put
$\epsilon=r-r_0$ and write the complete polynomial
$h(r_0+\epsilon)=\epsilon^m g(\epsilon)$, $g_0=g(0)\ne0$.
The local signed algebra and its residue functional are
\begin{equation}
 S_{u,r_0}=\C[\epsilon,t]/
   (\epsilon^m,\ t^2-mg_0\epsilon^{m-1}),\qquad
 \Lambda_{r_0}(R+tS)=[\epsilon^{m-1}]\frac{S}{g(\epsilon)}.
 \tag{RD9}
\end{equation}
The first relation gives the local root algebra by the
polynomial Chinese remainder construction; differentiating
$\epsilon^m g$ and reducing modulo $\epsilon^m$ gives
the second relation with its exact coefficient $mg_0$.
The last equation is the definition of the residue at $r_0$.
All coefficients of $g$ needed in the truncated inverse
remain in that functional.

Write $g^{-1}=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}b_j\epsilon^j$ modulo
$\epsilon^m$. These coefficients are finite exact expressions:
$b_0=g_0^{-1}$ and
$b_j=-g_0^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^j g_k b_{j-k}$.
In the order $(1,\epsilon,\ldots,\epsilon^{m-1},
t,t\epsilon,\ldots,t\epsilon^{m-1})$ the Gram matrix is
\begin{equation}
 B_{r_0}=\begin{pmatrix}0&H_{r_0}\\H_{r_0}&0\end{pmatrix},
 \quad (H_{r_0})_{ij}=
 \begin{cases}b_{m-1-i-j}&i+j\le m-1,\\0&i+j>m-1,\end{cases}
 \quad \det B_{r_0}=(-1)^m g_0^{-2m}.
 \tag{RD10}
\end{equation}
The determinant follows from the anti-triangular matrix:
$\det H_{r_0}=(-1)^{m(m-1)/2}g_0^{-m}$; swapping its two
blocks adds $(-1)^m$. A more explicit proof of invertibility
gives every coefficient of $F=R+tS$:
\begin{equation}
 R_j=\Lambda_{r_0}(F\,t\epsilon^{m-1-j}g),\qquad
 S_j=\Lambda_{r_0}(F\,\epsilon^{m-1-j}g),
 \quad 0\le j<m .
 \tag{RD11}
\end{equation}
Indeed the first odd coefficient is $R\epsilon^{m-1-j}g$,
and the second is $S\epsilon^{m-1-j}g$. Dividing by the
unchanged $g$ in RD9 extracts precisely the stated coefficient.
Thus every nonzero nilpotent has an explicitly specified
test vector with nonzero pairing.

On the other hand $t^4=0$ and
\begin{equation}
 2t^3F=2mg_0 R_0\,t\epsilon^{m-1},\qquad
 \operatorname{Tr}M_F=2mR_0,\qquad
 \ker M_{2t^3}=(\epsilon,t),\quad
 \operatorname{im}M_{2t^3}=\C\,t\epsilon^{m-1}.
 \tag{RD12}
\end{equation}
Here $t^4=m^2g_0^2\epsilon^{2m-2}=0$ because $m\ge2$.
Multiplication by $t^3=mg_0t\epsilon^{m-1}$ kills all
terms except $R_0$. The image vector has
$\Lambda_{r_0}(t\epsilon^{m-1})=g_0^{-1}$, proving
the trace formula and both displayed spaces. The trace
pairing is exactly $2mR_0P_0$ and has rank one on this
length-$2m$ algebra. For a simple root $h'(r_0)\ne0$,
the two signs are distinct, $t$ is a unit, and the trace
pairing has rank two. Therefore for a fibre with $n_1$
simple roots and $n_{\ge2}$ multiple roots the full trace
rank is $2n_1+n_{\ge2}$, while residue rank is always eight.

\section{The paired critical limit in the original quartet}
Retain $\gamma>0$, $\eta\in\{+1,-1\}$ and the original
factor $-1/2$. At $\delta=0$ put
\begin{equation}
 h_0(r)=-\tfrac12\bigl((r-\tfrac12)^2+\gamma^2\bigr)^2,
 \quad r_\eta=\tfrac12+i\eta\gamma,\quad
 g_\eta(\epsilon)=-\tfrac12(\epsilon+2i\eta\gamma)^2 .
 \tag{RD13}
\end{equation}
At each of the two roots the exact relations are
$g_0=2\gamma^2$, $g_1=-2i\eta\gamma$,
$t^2=4\gamma^2\epsilon$. Hence the two explicit mutually
inverse local homomorphisms identify the algebra with
$\C[t]/(t^4)$ by
$\epsilon\mapsto t^2/(4\gamma^2)$ and $t\mapsto t$.
This is the double-root case only; the triple and quadruple
root algebras remain the two-generator algebras RD9.
In the unchanged powers $(1,t,t^2,t^3)$ one obtains
\begin{equation}
 \Lambda_\eta(1)=\Lambda_\eta(t^2)=0,\quad
 \Lambda_\eta(t)=\frac{i\eta}{2\gamma^3},\quad
 \Lambda_\eta(t^3)=2,\qquad
 B_\eta=\begin{pmatrix}
 0&i\eta/(2\gamma^3)&0&2\\
 i\eta/(2\gamma^3)&0&2&0\\
 0&2&0&0\\2&0&0&0
 \end{pmatrix},\quad\det B_\eta=16.
 \tag{RD14}
\end{equation}
For example $\Lambda(t)=-g_1/g_0^2$ by RD9, and
$\Lambda(t^3)=4\gamma^2\Lambda(t\epsilon)=2$.
These evaluations prove every entry and the determinant.
Ordinary trace in this basis is $4$ times the product of
constant coefficients, so its rank is one in each cluster.

Set $X(\gamma)=\xi(1/2+i\gamma)$. The entire completion
and its reflection law are those of AR10--12, with the
human sources cited there. Reflection gives
$\xi(r_\eta)=X(\gamma)$ and differentiation gives
$\xi'(r_\eta)=-i\eta X'(\gamma)$. Thus its exact class is
\begin{equation}
 j_\eta\xi=X(\gamma)-\frac{i\eta X'(\gamma)}{4\gamma^2}t^2,
 \quad
 \Lambda_\eta((j_\eta\xi)t^3)=2X(\gamma),\quad
 \Lambda_\eta((j_\eta\xi)t)=
 \frac{i\eta X(\gamma)}{2\gamma^3}
       -\frac{i\eta X'(\gamma)}{2\gamma^2}.
 \tag{RD15}
\end{equation}
Every equality follows by reducing the Taylor series modulo
$\epsilon^2$ and using RD14. These two measurements recover
both $X$ and $X'$ without division by either:
\begin{equation}
 X=\tfrac12\Lambda_\eta((j_\eta\xi)t^3),\quad
 X'=2i\eta\gamma^2\left[
 \Lambda_\eta((j_\eta\xi)t)
 -\frac{i\eta}{4\gamma^3}\Lambda_\eta((j_\eta\xi)t^3)
 \right].
 \tag{RD16}
\end{equation}
The coefficient of $t^2$ is therefore not erased by passing
to the completed fibre. This statement makes no assumption
about whether a particular $X$ or $X'$ is zero. For any
entire $F$ of order $\nu$ at a root of multiplicity $m$,
its multiplication rank on RD9 is exactly
$2\max(m-\nu,0)$: multiplication by its unit Taylor factor
is invertible, and multiplication by $\epsilon^\nu$ has
rank $m-\nu$ on each of the two free parity summands when
$\nu<m$, and rank zero otherwise.

Both local measurements are literal measurements on the
whole eight-dimensional fibre. Put $d_\eta=2i\eta\gamma$,
$\epsilon_\eta=r-r_\eta$ and define its root projector by
\begin{equation}
 e_\eta(r)=\frac{(r-r_{-\eta})^2}{d_\eta^2}
                \left(1-\frac{2\epsilon_\eta}{d_\eta}\right)
                  \pmod{h_0}.
 \tag{RD17}
\end{equation}
It is $1$ modulo $\epsilon_\eta^2$ and $0$ modulo
$(r-r_{-\eta})^2$. The two ideals are coprime since
$d_\eta\ne0$, so $e_++e_-=1$, $e_\eta^2=e_\eta$ and
$e_+e_-=0$. These identities prove the direct product
decomposition, with the original signs retained.
For every $F$, $\Lambda_\eta(F)=\Lambda_u(e_\eta F)$.
Thus RD15 uses the global test vectors $e_\eta t^3$ and
$e_\eta t$, with all products reduced in the exact RD1 basis.

\section{Every dual vector in the two original conductor spaces}
Fix the original period and its four distinct labelled reference
roots, as in FC26--32. The reference quartet is held fixed while
$u$ varies in RD1; it is not replaced by the colliding roots.
Write $\mathcal U=\mathcal P_{v+3}/\mathcal P_{v-1}$,
$\mathcal W=\mathcal P_3$, and keep the exact maps
$X,Y,\Phi,\Psi,E,O$ of FC32 and SF2--3. In particular
$Y=T_AX$, $\Psi=T_A\Phi$, and $O$ is the original invertible
odd frame. In the actual target variable $S'$ the center is
$c_*=a_{\rm amp}/2-4$; retain $\kappa=c_*-1/2$.
The spectral coefficient map is
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal R_u:\mathcal W\oplus\mathcal W\longrightarrow S_u,
 \quad(p,q)\longmapsto p(r+\kappa)+tq(r+\kappa),\qquad
 \mathcal R_u^{-1}(R+tS)=(R(S'-\kappa),S(S'-\kappa)).
 \tag{RD18}
\end{equation}
The remainders $R,S$ have degree at most three, so the
two substitution maps compose to the identity in both
directions. Their coefficient matrix is
$D_\kappa=\operatorname{diag}(C_\kappa,C_\kappa)$ with
the full binomial matrix RW5; its determinant is one.

For the original eight signed coordinates $z=(\alpha,\beta)$ set
\begin{align}
 \mathcal J_W z&=(Y\alpha,\Psi(E\alpha+O\beta)),\nonumber\\
 \mathcal J_U z&=(X\alpha,\Phi(E\alpha+O\beta)),\qquad
 \mathcal J_W=(T_A\oplus T_A)\mathcal J_U,\nonumber\\
 Q_u&=D_\kappa
 \begin{pmatrix}Y&0\\\Psi E&\Psi O\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \mathcal Q_u=\mathcal R_u\mathcal J_W
      =\mathcal R_u(T_A\oplus T_A)\mathcal J_U .
 \tag{RD19}
\end{align}
The entries of $Y,\Psi$ in this matrix are in the original
target monomial basis. Its complete inverse on a class
$R+tS$ is
\begin{equation}
 \alpha=Y^{-1}C_{-\kappa}[R],\qquad
 \beta=O^{-1}\left(\Psi^{-1}C_{-\kappa}[S]-E\alpha\right).
 \tag{RD20}
\end{equation}
Square brackets here mean the cubic coefficient column.
Substitution in RD19 proves both inverse identities. Its
actual source state is, without changing its quotient norm,
$(T_A^{-1}R(S'-\kappa),T_A^{-1}S(S'-\kappa))$.
Every coefficient of this inverse is the finite recurrence
FC24 with its original moments and weights. Thus RD17 and
RD20 explicitly return every critical derivative test to
the original source, including its retained label component.

The residue and trace matrices in the signed coordinates are
\begin{equation}
 \widetilde B_u=Q_u^{\mathsf T}B_uQ_u,\quad
 \widetilde B_u^{-1}=Q_u^{-1}B_u^{-1}Q_u^{-\mathsf T},\quad
 \widetilde T_u=\widetilde B_u\,\widetilde D_u,\quad
 \widetilde D_u=Q_u^{-1}M_{2t^3}Q_u,\quad
 \det\widetilde B_u=\frac{(\det Y\det\Psi\det O)^2}{A^8}.
 \tag{RD21}
\end{equation}
These are transpose congruences because residue is bilinear.
All inverses exist for every $A\ne0$, including $\delta=0$
in RD13. In contrast, the exact kernel of the trace matrix
is $Q_u^{-1}\ker M_{2t^3}$, with every local component
given by RD12. No kernel of the original $T_A$ has been
introduced by this composition.

The original positive norms are retained separately. Set
$L_0=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}I&0\\E&O\end{smallmatrix}\right)$.
With the original full Gram matrices of RW14 and RW19,
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb H_W=L_0^*\operatorname{diag}(G_Y,G)L_0,
 \quad
 \mathbb H_U=L_0^*\operatorname{diag}(G_X,G_U)L_0,
 \quad
 \|\ell\|_{W^*}^2=\ell\mathbb H_W^{-1}\ell^*,\quad
 \|\ell\|_{U^*}^2=\ell\mathbb H_U^{-1}\ell^* .
 \tag{RD22}
\end{equation}
Here $\ell$ is any one of the exact linear rows
$f^{\mathsf T}\widetilde B_u$ furnished by RD11 or RD17--21.
The dual norm follows by writing a positive Gram as $H^{1/2}H^{1/2}$
and applying Cauchy--Schwarz to $\ell H^{-1/2}$, with equality
on its adjoint column. This derivation changes no input norm.
It includes all Gamma masses, original centers and moments
already evaluated in FC28--30 and RW19--20.

The existing full Weil form also has an exact expression
through this nondegenerate residue pairing. For its actual
Hermitian matrix $\mathbb W$ from RW14, define the
conjugate-linear map
\begin{equation}
 \mathscr A_u(z)=\widetilde B_u^{-1}\mathbb W^{\mathsf T}\bar z,
 \qquad
 \mathcal B_u(\mathcal Q_u\mathscr A_u(z),\mathcal Q_u w)
       =z^*\mathbb Ww.
 \tag{RD23}
\end{equation}
Transpose the displayed formula and use the symmetry of
$\widetilde B_u$ to prove the equality. Since the latter
matrix is invertible and $\mathbb W$ is Hermitian,
$\ker\mathscr A_u=\ker\mathbb W$; its exact kernel is
RW17, and its rank equals that of $\mathbb W$.
Equation RD23 is a proved correspondence, not a claim that
bilinear nondegeneracy implies Hermitian positivity.

\section{The remaining root charts and the seven-point fibre}
No zero of $A$ is discarded from the finite completion.
On the open $h(p)\ne0$, $p\in\{0,1,-1,2\}$, use the
actual determinant-one chart FS7:
\begin{equation}
 R=(p-r)^{-1},\quad T=t/(p-r),\quad
 h_p(R)=R^4h(p-R^{-1})=aR^4+bR^3+cR^2+dR+e .
 \tag{RD24}
\end{equation}
Its leading coefficient is $a=h(p)\ne0$ and its signed
equation remains $T^2=h_p'(R)$. Define
$\Lambda_p(U+TV)=[R^3](V\bmod h_p)/a$.
The complete matrices in this chart are
\begin{equation}
 H_p=\begin{pmatrix}
 0&0&0&a^{-1}\\0&0&a^{-1}&-ba^{-2}\\
 0&a^{-1}&-ba^{-2}&b^2a^{-3}-ca^{-2}\\
 a^{-1}&-ba^{-2}&b^2a^{-3}-ca^{-2}&-b^3a^{-4}+2bca^{-3}-da^{-2}
 \end{pmatrix},\quad
 H_p^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}d&c&b&a\\c&b&a&0\\b&a&0&0\\a&0&0&0\end{pmatrix}.
 \tag{RD25}
\end{equation}
The same three remainder recurrences prove every entry and
their product is the identity. The signed Gram is
$B_p=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&H_p\\H_p&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$,
$\det B_p=a^{-8}$. The trace map is again exactly $M_{2T^3}$.
On the overlap with $A\ne0$, let $\iota$ substitute
$r=p-1/R$, $t=T/R$. The derivative identity in FS7 proves
that this is an algebra isomorphism, with the displayed
inverse, and the residue change of variable gives
\begin{equation}
 \Lambda_u(F)=\Lambda_p(R^3\iota(F)).
 \tag{RD26}
\end{equation}
Indeed $dr/h=R^2dR/h_p$, while the odd coefficient of
$\iota(F)$ is $S(p-1/R)/R$. Multiplication by $R^3$
therefore gives precisely the transformed residue. On this
overlap $R$ is a unit, so the transition is invertible.
This identifies the two dualities with the exact factor
$R^3$ retained.

There is also a full physical coefficient receiver on this
chart, including $R=0$. For $f\in\mathcal P_3$ define
\begin{equation}
 (Z_pf)(R)=R^3 f(p+\kappa-R^{-1}),\qquad
 f(S')=(p+\kappa-S')^3
       (Z_pf)((p+\kappa-S')^{-1}).
 \tag{RD27}
\end{equation}
Each expression is a polynomial of degree at most three;
the formulas compose to the identity by substitution.
For every monomial its forward image is exactly
$R^{3-j}((p+\kappa)R-1)^j$, $0\le j\le3$.
Reversing the column order gives diagonal $(-1)^j$;
both resulting signs are $(-1)^6=1$, so $\det Z_p=1$.
Thus $(f,g)\mapsto Z_pf+T Z_pg$ is an isomorphism
$\mathcal W^2\to S_{u,p}$ even at the infinity root.
Composing with $\mathcal J_W$ and its exact inverse RD20,
using $Z_p$ in place of $C_\kappa$, proves every analogue
of RD19--22 with $B_p$ and determinant denominator $a^8$.

This coefficient receiver is not silently identified with
the algebra change of variable. On their overlap its two
parity parts are $R^3\iota(F_{\mathrm{even}})$ and
$R^4\iota(F_{\mathrm{odd}})$. Therefore for two physical inputs
with finite-chart images $F,G$, the two pulled-back pairings
are related by the explicit invertible multiplier
\begin{equation}
 \Lambda_p(F_pG_p)
       =\Lambda_u((p-r)^{-4}FG).
 \tag{RD28}
\end{equation}
The odd product acquires $R^7$, and RD26 removes $R^3$,
leaving $R^4=(p-r)^{-4}$, which proves this identity.
The five opens cover the entire target by FS6. Hence these
formulas supply receivers and their exact comparisons on
every fibre of the original finite completion.

At the original target $(A,B,C,D)=(0,0,-1,0)$ take $p=2$.
Then the full polynomial, roots and residue determinant are
\begin{equation}
 h_2(R)=6R^4-11R^3+6R^2-R,\quad
 R\in\{0,\tfrac13,\tfrac12,1\},\quad
 \det B_2=6^{-8},\quad
 \det T_2=256\cdot4^3/6^{14}.
 \tag{RD29}
\end{equation}
Expansion of $(2R-1)^3R-(2R-1)R^3$ proves the polynomial.
Its factorization $R(R-1)(2R-1)(3R-1)$ proves the roots;
their difference product gives discriminant $4$.
At $R=0$, $T^2=h_2'(0)=-1$ gives both $T=\pm i$.
FS7 sends these to the two original infinity signs;
$T=-i$ is the original affine state and $T=i$ is the
omitted state. Thus all eight completed states, the seven
original affine states and the exact missing sign are
present in the same invertible residue calculation.

\section{Complete original-source inverse constants and marked return}


The finite signed-root algebra and its exact trace-to-residue
multiplier are those of RD1--29 and the received continuation
RE1--30 \cite{Fable037,ReceivedRE}. We derive the complete inverse singular
constants for every retained positive coefficient Gram, then
evaluate that Gram through the full original conductor inverse.
The original fixed conductor, its moments and both Gamma centers
are retained. The Gamma polynomial dictionary is the one credited
to the human authors of DLMF Chapter 18 in WCF1--6
\cite{WCF,DLMF18Source}; its application to the original conductor is
FC20--24 and NH26--34 \cite{Fable037}. The new calculation here is the
arbitrary-Gram singular decomposition and its exact original-source
specialization, rather than a new orthogonality result.

\section{The full signed multiplier with its complex phase}
Fix the original reference period and its conductor throughout.
Independently vary the real collision parameter $w=\delta^2>0$,
keeping $g=\gamma^2>0$ fixed. In the unchanged coordinate
$x=r-1/2$, set
\begin{equation}
 h_w(x)=-\tfrac12\bigl(x^4+2(g-w)x^2+(g+w)^2\bigr),\qquad
 S_w=\C[x,t]/(h_w,t^2-h_w'),\qquad
 \mathbf e=(1,x,x^2,x^3,t,tx,tx^2,tx^3).
 \tag{RS1}
\end{equation}
The factor $-1/2$ is unchanged: after replacing $x$ by $r-1/2$
the coefficient of $r^3$ is precisely one. The matrix of
multiplication by $x$ in each parity summand and the derivative
multiplier are
\begin{equation}
 X_w=\begin{pmatrix}
 0&0&0&-(g+w)^2\\1&0&0&0\\0&1&0&-2(g-w)\\0&0&1&0
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad
 H_w=-2X_w\bigl(X_w^2+(g-w)I\bigr).
 \tag{RS2}
\end{equation}
These follow by reducing $x^4$ in the exact algebra RS1 and
differentiating its unchanged $h_w$. In particular,
\begin{equation}
 H_w^2=-16gwX_w^2,\qquad
 \mathsf J_w=M_{2t^3}=2\begin{pmatrix}0&H_w^2\\H_w&0\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad c=\frac{1+i}{\sqrt2},\qquad \Theta_w=\mathsf J_w/c.
 \tag{RS3}
\end{equation}
To verify the first identity, the relation RS1 gives
$(X_w^2+(g-w)I)^2=-4gwI$; multiplying by $4X_w^2$
gives the asserted expression. The second follows by cubing
$M_t=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&H_w\\I&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$.
The phase $c$ is the reflected-residue convention RE10--12:
$x^\sharp=-x$, $t^\sharp=it$, with conjugation of coefficients.
It respects $t^2=h_w'(x)$ and has square one. In that convention
the reflected trace is exactly the reflected residue composed
with $\Theta_w$, not with a multiplier in which $c$ is omitted.
We retain this $c$ in every operator identity below.

Since $\det X_w=(g+w)^2$, RS3 gives for $w>0$
\begin{equation}
 \mathsf J_w^{-1}
 =\frac12\begin{pmatrix}0&H_w^{-1}\\H_w^{-2}&0\end{pmatrix}
 =\begin{pmatrix}
 0&\dfrac{X_w^2+(g-w)I}{16gwX_w}\\[4pt]
 -\dfrac{X_w^{-2}}{32gw}&0
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \Theta_w^{-1}=c\mathsf J_w^{-1}.
 \tag{RS4}
\end{equation}
For the upper-right equality multiply by $H_w$ and use
$(X_w^2+(g-w)I)^2=-4gwI$. The lower-left equality is the
inverse of the first identity RS3. These checks prove both
inverse products and preserve every complex phase.

At $w=0$ define $X_0=X_w|_{w=0}$ and
\begin{equation}
 N=X_0^2+gI=
 \begin{pmatrix}g&0&-g^2&0\\0&g&0&-g^2\\1&0&-g&0\\0&1&0&-g\end{pmatrix},
 \quad H_0=-2X_0N,\quad
 N^2=0,\quad\rank N=\rank H_0=2.
 \tag{RS5}
\end{equation}
The first and third columns of each parity block are proportional,
and the first two columns are independent, so $N$ has rank two.
Because $X_0$ is invertible, $H_0$ has rank two as well.
Since $X_0^2=-gI+N$ and $N^2=0$, its inverse square is
$X_0^{-2}=-(gI+N)/g^2$. Therefore RS4 gives the full matrix limit
\begin{equation}
 w\Theta_w^{-1}\longrightarrow
 c\begin{pmatrix}0&R_1\\R_2&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 R_1=\frac{H_0}{32g^2},\qquad R_2=\frac{gI+N}{32g^3}.
 \tag{RS6}
\end{equation}
For the first block, $H_0X_0^{-2}=-H_0/g$, since $H_0N=0$.
For the second block use the displayed inverse square. This also
proves that the residue of the inverse has rank six.

\section{Every singular constant for an arbitrary retained Gram}
Let $G$ be any fixed positive definite Hermitian four-by-four
matrix in the exact increasing powers $(1,x,x^2,x^3)$.
No parity condition is imposed. Endow $S_w$ with
$G_8=\diag(G,G)$ in the order RS1. For a four-dimensional
operator $A$, put
\begin{equation}
 K_A=G^{-1}A^*GA,\qquad
 a_A=\tr K_A,\qquad
 b_A=\frac{(\tr K_A)^2-\tr(K_A^2)}2.
 \tag{RS7}
\end{equation}
These expressions are computed from the original Gram.
$K_A$ is similar to the positive semidefinite Hermitian matrix
$(G^{1/2}AG^{-1/2})^*(G^{1/2}AG^{-1/2})$, so its eigenvalues
are the squared singular values in that Gram. For $A=N,H_0$
exactly two are positive. Thus the following are all of their
positive singular values, in decreasing order:
\begin{equation}
 \chi_{1,2}=
 \sqrt{\frac{a_N\pm\sqrt{a_N^2-4b_N}}2},\qquad
 \eta_{1,2}=
 \sqrt{\frac{a_{H_0}\pm\sqrt{a_{H_0}^2-4b_{H_0}}}2}.
 \tag{RS8}
\end{equation}
Both inner discriminants are nonnegative: for two positive
eigenvalues $\lambda_1,\lambda_2$ they equal
$(\lambda_1-\lambda_2)^2$. Their products $b_A$ are strictly
positive. Thus all radicals in RS8 are well defined, including
equality of the two singular values.

There is an exact two-by-two evaluation of every invariant in RS8.
It keeps every entry of $G$ and of $G^{-1}$:
\begin{equation}
 V=\begin{pmatrix}g&0\\0&g\\1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 R=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&-g&0\\0&1&0&-g\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 J_g=\begin{pmatrix}0&-g\\1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 Q=V^*GV,\quad P=RG^{-1}R^*.
 \tag{RS9}
\end{equation}
Both $P,Q$ are positive definite. Matrix multiplication gives
$N=VR$, $RV=0$, $X_0V=VJ_g$ and $H_0=-2VJ_gR$.
The nonzero eigenvalues of products $AB$ and $BA$ agree, with
algebraic multiplicity: for a nonzero eigenvalue, $B$ maps its
generalized eigenspace injectively to that of $BA$, with inverse
obtained from the finite inverse of $AB$ on that eigenspace.
Applying this to the displayed factorizations, or taking the
coefficient identity $\det(I+zAB)=\det(I+zBA)$, yields
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 a_N&=\tr(PQ),& b_N&=\det P\det Q,\\
 a_{H_0}&=4\tr(PJ_g^*QJ_g),&
 b_{H_0}&=16g^2\det P\det Q,\qquad
 \eta_1\eta_2=4g\chi_1\chi_2.
 \end{aligned}
 \tag{RS10}
\end{equation}
For example $\det J_g=g$, so the last determinant factor is
$4^2|\det J_g|^2=16g^2$. These are finite two-by-two formulas,
not unresolved singular-value problems. To make their entry use
explicit, indices $0,1,2,3$ refer to the original monomials and
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 Q_{11}&=g^2G_{00}+g(G_{02}+G_{20})+G_{22},\\
 Q_{12}&=g^2G_{01}+gG_{03}+gG_{21}+G_{23},\\
 Q_{22}&=g^2G_{11}+g(G_{13}+G_{31})+G_{33},\\
 P_{11}&=(G^{-1})_{00}-g((G^{-1})_{02}+(G^{-1})_{20})
                                      +g^2(G^{-1})_{22},\\
 P_{12}&=(G^{-1})_{01}-g(G^{-1})_{03}-g(G^{-1})_{21}
                                      +g^2(G^{-1})_{23},\\
 P_{22}&=(G^{-1})_{11}-g((G^{-1})_{13}+(G^{-1})_{31})
                                      +g^2(G^{-1})_{33},
 \quad P_{21}=\overline{P_{12}},\quad Q_{21}=\overline{Q_{12}}.
 \end{aligned}
 \tag{RS11}
\end{equation}

We next evaluate all four singular values of $R_2$.
Let $\mathcal L=\im N$. Its dimension is two and $N^2=0$,
so $\mathcal L=\ker N$. The $G$-orthogonal decomposition
$\C^4=\mathcal L\oplus\mathcal L^{\perp_G}$ gives
\begin{equation}
 N=\begin{pmatrix}0&S\\0&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 gI+N=\begin{pmatrix}gI&S\\0&gI\end{pmatrix},
 \tag{RS12}
\end{equation}
where $S:\mathcal L^{\perp_G}\to\mathcal L$ is invertible.
Use orthonormal bases on these two actual metric subspaces.
Diagonalize the positive matrix $S^*S$, then take the normalized
images under $S$ as the basis on $\mathcal L$. This makes $S$
diagonal with positive entries $\chi_1,\chi_2$ by isometries.
It does not alter the original metric. After interleaving the
two pairs, $gI+N$ is the orthogonal direct sum of
$\left(\begin{smallmatrix}g&\chi_j\\0&g\end{smallmatrix}\right)$.
The squared singular-value sum of that two-by-two matrix is
$2g^2+\chi_j^2$, and its singular-value product is $g^2$.
The two positive numbers
$\bigl(\sqrt{4g^2+\chi_j^2}\pm\chi_j\bigr)/2$
have exactly that sum of squares and product, so they are
its two singular values. Consequently the four constants are
\begin{equation}
 \zeta_{j,\pm}
 =\frac{\sqrt{4g^2+\chi_j^2}\pm\chi_j}{64g^3}>0,
 \qquad j=1,2.
 \tag{RS13}
\end{equation}

Form the six positive numbers and the two finite constants
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal C=
 \left\{\frac{\eta_1}{32g^2},\frac{\eta_2}{32g^2},
 \zeta_{1,+},\zeta_{1,-},\zeta_{2,+},\zeta_{2,-}\right\},
 \qquad d_1=\frac1{2\eta_2},\quad d_2=\frac1{2\eta_1}.
 \tag{RS14}
\end{equation}
Write $c_1\ge\cdots\ge c_6$ for the decreasing ordering of
the displayed six numbers; $d_1\ge d_2$. Every ordered singular
value of $\Theta_w^{-1}$ in $G_8$ is now determined:
\begin{equation}
 \sigma_j(\Theta_w^{-1})=\frac{c_j}{w}(1+o(1)),\quad 1\le j\le6,
 \qquad
 \sigma_7(\Theta_w^{-1})=d_1(1+o(1)),\quad
 \sigma_8(\Theta_w^{-1})=d_2(1+o(1)).
 \tag{RS15}
\end{equation}
Here is a proof covering the finite constants as well as the
divergent ones. In $\diag(G,G)$, the squared singular spectrum
of a block matrix $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&A\\B&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$
is the union of the squared singular spectra of $A$ and $B$,
because its metric adjoint times itself is block diagonal.
For the upper-right block of RS4 the singular values are the
reciprocals of twice the singular values of $H_w$. The two
largest singular values of $H_w$ tend to $\eta_1,\eta_2$,
so two inverse singular values tend to $d_2,d_1$.
Multiplying this block by $w$ gives $R_1$, which has precisely
two positive singular values $\eta_j/(32g^2)$. Thus its other
two singular values diverge with those constants. The lower-left
block times $w$ tends to the invertible $R_2$, whose four
singular values are RS13. Singular values are continuous under
matrix convergence: the minimum-maximum description of the
singular values gives their change bounded by the operator norm
of the difference. Finally $|c|=1$, so its unchanged phase in
RS4 changes none of these singular values. This proves RS15.

\section{Every exterior rank and the exact determinant}
The exterior norm in the metric induced by $G_8$ is the product
of the largest singular values. Indeed orthonormal singular
bases induce orthonormal wedge bases, and the matrix entries
on those bases are all corresponding products. Define
\begin{equation}
 C_0=1,\qquad C_r=\prod_{j=1}^r c_j
 =\max_{\substack{I\subset\{1,\ldots,6\}\\|I|=r}}
                         \prod_{j\in I}\mathcal C_j,
 \quad1\le r\le6.
 \tag{RS16}
\end{equation}
The maximum is a finite explicit expression in the radicals
RS8 and RS13 and makes no assumption about their ordering for
different original moments. For $r=6$ the product evaluates to
\begin{equation}
 \zeta_{j,+}\zeta_{j,-}=\frac1{2^{10}g^4},\qquad
 C_6=\frac{\eta_1\eta_2}{2^{30}g^{12}}
     =\frac{\sqrt{b_{H_0}}}{2^{30}g^{12}},\qquad
 C_6d_1=\frac{\eta_1}{2^{31}g^{12}}.
 \tag{RS17}
\end{equation}
The first equality follows by subtracting the two squares in
the numerator of RS13. Multiplying its two instances and
the first two entries of RS14 proves the others.

The determinant is also exact for every $w>0$:
\begin{equation}
 \det H_w=256g^2w^2(g+w)^2,\qquad
 \det\Theta_w=2^{32}g^6w^6(g+w)^6,\qquad
 \det\Theta_w^{-1}=\frac1{2^{32}g^6w^6(g+w)^6}.
 \tag{RS18}
\end{equation}
For the first formula, the roots of the monic quartic in RS1
are $\varepsilon\sqrt w+i\eta\sqrt g$, with independent signs.
At each root, the factor $x^2+g-w$ equals
$2i\varepsilon\eta\sqrt{gw}$. Their product is $16g^2w^2$;
$\det(-2X_w)=16(g+w)^2$. Multiplication gives the first
identity. The determinant of the two swapped four-by-four
blocks of $\mathsf J_w$ is $2^8(\det H_w)^3$, with positive
block-swap sign. Also $c^8=1$. This proves both remaining
identities, including their phases.

Combining RS15--18 gives every inverse exterior asymptote:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\begin{aligned}
 \|\wedge^0\Theta_w^{-1}\|_{G_8}&=1,\\
 \|\wedge^r\Theta_w^{-1}\|_{G_8}&\sim C_rw^{-r},&&1\le r\le6,\\
 \|\wedge^7\Theta_w^{-1}\|_{G_8}&\sim
                       \frac{\eta_1}{2^{31}g^{12}}w^{-6},\\
 \|\wedge^8\Theta_w^{-1}\|_{G_8}&=
                       \frac1{2^{32}g^6w^6(g+w)^6}.
 \end{aligned}}
 \tag{RS19}
\end{equation}
The complete leading product is
$C_6d_1d_2=1/(2^{32}g^{12})$, agreeing with RS18.
For the original variable $\delta$, every exponent is
$2\min(r,6)$, with exactly the constants displayed above.

\section{The exact Gram furnished by the original source}
We now evaluate $G$, rather than leave it as an auxiliary
positive matrix. Keep the original conductor $L=T_A$ on
\begin{equation}
 U=\mathcal P_{v+3}/\mathcal P_{v-1},\qquad
 W=\mathcal P_3,\qquad
 c_*=a_{\rm amp}/2-4,\quad c_0=c_*+4,\quad
 b=s/2>0,\quad M=(2\pi)^{s/2}.
 \tag{RS20}
\end{equation}
The source has its minimum quotient norm at center $c_0$;
the target has its original Gamma norm at center $c_*$.
Both use the same fixed original order $s$ in the allowed
programme domain. In target coordinates
$y=(S'-c_*)/i$, $x=S'-c_*=iy$.
Thus the spectral coordinate $x=r-1/2$ is sent by the actual
dictionary $r=S'-\kappa$, $\kappa=c_*-1/2$, to exactly
$S'-c_*$. No center is set to zero in the original spaces.

Write $\mu_n=E_A^{(n)}(0)$ and let $v$ be its actual
vanishing order, so $\mu_j=0$ for $j<v$ and $\mu_v\ne0$.
To distinguish the conductor coefficients from $g=\gamma^2$,
use $\mathfrak g_j$ for the coefficients of its exact FC20 unit:
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 z(q)=-i\arctan q,\qquad
 \mathfrak g(q)=q^{-v}e^{-4z(q)}E_A(z(q)),\\
 \nu_{v+j}=\sum_{k=0}^{j}\binom{v+j}{v+k}(-4)^{j-k}\mu_{v+k},\\
 \mathfrak g_0=(-i)^v\mu_v/v!,\qquad
 \mathfrak g_1=(-i)^{v+1}\nu_{v+1}/(v+1)!,\\
 \mathfrak g_2=(-i)^{v+2}\nu_{v+2}/(v+2)!-(v/3)\mathfrak g_0,\\
 \mathfrak g_3=(-i)^{v+3}\nu_{v+3}/(v+3)!-((v+1)/3)\mathfrak g_1,\\
 \xi_0=\mathfrak g_0^{-1},\quad
 \xi_1=-\mathfrak g_1\mathfrak g_0^{-2},\quad
 \xi_2=\mathfrak g_1^2\mathfrak g_0^{-3}-\mathfrak g_2\mathfrak g_0^{-2},\\
 \xi_3=-\mathfrak g_1^3\mathfrak g_0^{-4}
       +2\mathfrak g_1\mathfrak g_2\mathfrak g_0^{-3}
       -\mathfrak g_3\mathfrak g_0^{-2}.
 \end{gathered}
 \tag{RS21}
\end{equation}
The finite moment formula for $\nu$ follows by multiplying
$e^{-4z}E_A(z)$ as power series. Since
$z(q)^n=(-i)^nq^n(1-nq^2/3+O(q^4))$, it gives every
$\mathfrak g_j$ displayed through degree three. Multiplying
the two cubic truncations $\sum\mathfrak g_jq^j$ and
$\sum\xi_jq^j$ gives $1$ modulo $q^4$, proving all inverse
coefficients with their original phases.

For completeness the original orthonormal source polynomials
and their minimum quotient representatives are
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 p_0(y)=1,\quad p_1(y)=y,\quad
 p_{n+1}(y)=yp_n(y)-n(n-1+b)p_{n-1}(y),\\
 \phi_n(S)=\frac{p_n((S-c_0)/i)}{\sqrt{Mn!(b)_n}},\qquad
 [\phi_v],\ldots,[\phi_{v+3}]\text{ an orthonormal frame of }U,\\
 \rho_n=\sqrt{n!/(b)_n},\qquad
 k_j=\rho_{v+j}^{-2}=(b)_{v+j}/(v+j)!,\quad0\le j\le3.
 \end{gathered}
 \tag{RS22}
\end{equation}
The orthogonality and original measure are the fixed Gamma
dictionary of \cite{WCF,DLMF18Source}; its squared norms include $M$.
Each listed polynomial is orthogonal to $\mathcal P_{v-1}$.
Consequently Pythagoras proves that its linear combinations
are precisely the minimum-norm representatives of their classes.

In the original source and target orthonormal frames FC24 has
entry $\xi_{j-r}\rho_j/\rho_{v+r}$ for $0\le r\le j\le3$.
To evaluate its action on monomials, the target orthonormal
coordinate columns of $(1,y,y^2,y^3)$ are
\begin{equation}
 V_W=\begin{pmatrix}
 \sqrt M&0&b\sqrt M&0\\
 0&\sqrt{Mb}&0&(3b+2)\sqrt{Mb}\\
 0&0&\sqrt{2Mb(b+1)}&0\\
 0&0&0&\sqrt{6Mb(b+1)(b+2)}
 \end{pmatrix}.
 \tag{RS23}
\end{equation}
Indeed $p_2=y^2-b$ and $p_3=y^3-(3b+2)y$ follow
from RS22. Multiplying these columns by the complete FC24
inverse, and using
$\rho_j\sqrt{Mj!(b)_j}=\sqrt M\,j!$, gives
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 F=\begin{pmatrix}
 \xi_0&\xi_1&b\xi_0+2\xi_2&(3b+2)\xi_1+6\xi_3\\
 0&\xi_0&2\xi_1&(3b+2)\xi_0+6\xi_2\\
 0&0&2\xi_0&6\xi_1\\0&0&0&6\xi_0
 \end{pmatrix},\quad D_i=\diag(1,i,-1,-i),\\
 U_x=\sqrt M\diag(\sqrt{k_0},\sqrt{k_1},\sqrt{k_2},\sqrt{k_3})FD_i,\\
 L^{-1}(S'-c_*)^j=\sum_{r=0}^3(U_x)_{rj}[\phi_{v+r}],\qquad
 G_{U,x}=U_x^*U_x=M D_i^*F^*\diag(k_0,k_1,k_2,k_3)FD_i.
 \end{gathered}
 \tag{RS24}
\end{equation}
The additional $D_i$ is required because $x^j=i^jy^j$.
Thus $G_{U,x}$ is the full original source Gram in precisely
the $x$ coefficients of RS1. The subscript records this receiver;
the programme Gram $\Phi^*\langle\, ,\,\rangle_U\Phi$ in
original ES coordinates keeps its existing notation.

Its entries and inverse can be evaluated without a numerical
matrix inversion. In addition to
$(G_{U,x})_{pq}=M(-i)^p i^q
\sum_{r=0}^{\min(p,q)}k_r\overline{F_{rp}}F_{rq}$,
the complete inverse of $F$ is
\begin{equation}
 B_F=\begin{pmatrix}
 \mathfrak g_0&\mathfrak g_1&\mathfrak g_2-b\mathfrak g_0/2
                       &\mathfrak g_3-b\mathfrak g_1/2\\
 0&\mathfrak g_0&\mathfrak g_1
                       &\mathfrak g_2-(3b+2)\mathfrak g_0/6\\
 0&0&\mathfrak g_0/2&\mathfrak g_1/2\\
 0&0&0&\mathfrak g_0/6
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad
 G_{U,x}^{-1}=M^{-1}D_i^*B_F\diag(k_j^{-1})B_F^*D_i.
 \tag{RS25}
\end{equation}
To prove the inverse, write
$F=T_3(\xi)V_0$, where $V_0$ has diagonal $(1,1,2,6)$,
entry $(0,2)$ equal to $b$ and entry $(1,3)$ equal to $3b+2$.
All other off-diagonal entries vanish. The reciprocal identity
in RS21 gives $T_3(\xi)^{-1}=T_3(\mathfrak g)$, and direct
upper-triangular multiplication gives $V_0^{-1}T_3(\mathfrak g)=B_F$.
Thus $FB_F=B_FF=I$, proving RS25. All original cross terms
remain in both products RS24--25. Positivity and invertibility
also follow from the exact determinant
\begin{equation}
 \det G_{U,x}=144M^4 k_0k_1k_2k_3|\xi_0|^8>0.
 \tag{RS26}
\end{equation}
In fact all four leading minors are respectively
$Mk_0|\xi_0|^2$, $M^2k_0k_1|\xi_0|^4$,
$4M^3k_0k_1k_2|\xi_0|^6$ and RS26, by the triangular
diagonal of $F$. The phases in $D_i$ have modulus one.

Substituting RS24--25 into RS9--11 and then RS8, RS13--19
therefore evaluates all eight source singular constants in
finite original moments, Gamma weights and real radicals.
Every matrix used in this substitution has all its entries
displayed. The common $M$ cancels from $G_{U,x}^{-1}A^*G_{U,x}A$
because it occurs on both sides of the same operator norm;
it remains unchanged in the source norms RS24 and RS26.

\section{The exact conductor receiver and the completed estimate}
Define the coefficient receivers, on each of the two parity
summands, by
\begin{equation}
 C_Wp=p(S'-c_*),\qquad C_Up=L^{-1}p(S'-c_*),\qquad
 \mathcal C_W=C_W\oplus C_W,\quad
 \mathcal C_U=C_U\oplus C_U.
 \tag{RS27}
\end{equation}
They are isomorphisms from the coefficient space of $S_w$
to $W^2$ and $U^2$ respectively; the inverse of $C_W$ is
$q(S')\mapsto q(x+c_*)$, and that of $C_U$ is
$u\mapsto (Lu)(x+c_*)$. The full inverse matrix in the original
source frame is supplied by RS24--25. The original relation is
$(L\oplus L)\mathcal C_U=\mathcal C_W$.
With $G=G_{U,x}$ in RS7--19, $\mathcal C_U$ is an isometry
from $\diag(G_{U,x},G_{U,x})$ onto the original quotient direct sum,
by its proved Gram RS24. The transported operation and its
inverse are consequently
\begin{equation}
 \Theta_{U,w}=\mathcal C_U\Theta_w\mathcal C_U^{-1},\quad
 \Theta_{U,w}^{-1}=\mathcal C_U\Theta_w^{-1}\mathcal C_U^{-1},\quad
 (L\oplus L)\Theta_{U,w}=\Theta_{W,w}(L\oplus L),
 \quad\Theta_{W,w}=\mathcal C_W\Theta_w\mathcal C_W^{-1}.
 \tag{RS28}
\end{equation}
Substitution and cancellation of the stated inverse maps prove
all identities. The singular constants of $\Theta_{U,w}^{-1}$
are exactly RS14--15 evaluated at the explicit RS24 Gram,
and every original-source exterior norm is exactly RS19 with
that substitution. No condition-number bound replaces a source
constant. The determinant RS18 is unchanged by this similarity.

As a direct equation-(11) consequence, for every rank $1\le r\le8$
the product
\begin{equation}
 w^{\min(r,6)}\|\wedge^r\Theta_{U,w}^{-1}\|_U
 \longrightarrow
 \begin{cases}
 C_r(G_{U,x}),&1\le r\le6,\\
 \eta_1(G_{U,x})/(2^{31}g^{12}),&r=7,\\
 1/(2^{32}g^{12}),&r=8.
 \end{cases}
 \tag{RS29}
\end{equation}
Every limit is strictly positive by RS8 and RS13. Hence this
specific completed trace-to-residue operation has no uniform
inverse exterior bound as the paired collision is approached.
The locus of this loss is the explicit multiplier RS3, whose
rank at $w=0$ is two. The original $L$ remains the fixed
isomorphism with determinant FC23; its nonzero leading moment
$\mu_v$ is retained in RS21--26. Equations RS27--28 are the exact
map between these two statements and do not change the original
conductor's order.


\section{The same singular constants in the complete marked receiver}
We now prove the exact change of coordinates from RS27 to RD19,
including the mixed metric. The original reference period and its
distinct quartet are fixed throughout. Its ordered labels remain
$(\mathrm M,\mathrm N,\mathrm T,\mathrm E)$, with both signs at
each label. The varying quartic is RS1; its parameter $w=\delta^2$
does not replace that reference period. The marked frames
$K,X,Y,\Phi,\Psi,E,O$ are exactly FC26--32 and SF2--3.
In particular $Y=\Psi K$, $X=\Phi K$, $Y=T_AX$ and
$\Psi=T_A\Phi$. All their original phases and entries are retained.
The human polynomial provenance remains Alp\"oge's announcement
and Tao's account \cite{Tao}; the four labels and their unchanged
marking come from the ES source \cite{ES488}.

For a scalar $a$, define the complete cubic substitution matrix
\begin{equation}
 C_a=\begin{pmatrix}1&a&a^2&a^3\\0&1&2a&3a^2\\
 0&0&1&3a\\0&0&0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 [p(x+a)]=C_a[p(x)],\quad C_aC_b=C_{a+b},\quad C_a^{-1}=C_{-a}.
 \tag{MR1}
\end{equation}
The coefficient of $x^i$ in $(x+a)^j$ is
$\binom ji a^{j-i}$, proving the first identity. Substitution
twice proves the composition and inverse without discarding
any original center. Retain $c_*=a_{\rm amp}/2-4$ and
$\kappa=c_*-1/2$. In RS1 the coordinate is $x=r-1/2$, so
the full $r$-coefficient matrix $Q_r$ of RD19 becomes
\begin{equation}
 \begin{split}
 Z&=C_{c_*}\Psi,\\
 Q_x&=\operatorname{diag}(C_{1/2},C_{1/2})Q_r
   =\begin{pmatrix}C_{c_*}Y&0\\C_{c_*}\Psi E&C_{c_*}\Psi O\end{pmatrix}
   =\operatorname{diag}(Z,Z)\begin{pmatrix}K&0\\E&O\end{pmatrix},\\
 Q_x^{-1}&=\begin{pmatrix}
 K^{-1}Z^{-1}&0\\-O^{-1}EK^{-1}Z^{-1}&O^{-1}Z^{-1}
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \det Q_x=(\det\Psi)^2\det K\det O\ne0.
 \end{split}\tag{MR2}
\end{equation}
Indeed $C_{1/2}C_\kappa=C_{c_*}$ and $Y=\Psi K$.
Multiplication of the two displayed block matrices proves
both inverse products. The original odd frame $O$ is
invertible by SE6; all remaining factors are the original
FC isomorphisms. These facts prove the last assertion with
the actual determinant, rather than selecting a new frame.

Use the two coefficient receivers $\mathcal C_U,\mathcal C_W$
of RS27. The exact identities with the original marked maps are
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal J_U=\mathcal C_UQ_x,\qquad
 \mathcal J_W=\mathcal C_WQ_x,\qquad
 (T_A\oplus T_A)\mathcal J_U=\mathcal J_W.
 \tag{MR3}
\end{equation}
For example, $C_WC_{c_*}Y\alpha=Y\alpha$ since
$(C_{c_*}p)(S'-c_*)=p(S')$. Applying the original $T_A^{-1}$
gives $X\alpha$. The second component gives
$\Phi(E\alpha+O\beta)$ by the same substitutions. This proves
each component of MR3 and both directions of the correspondence.

Write $G_{U,x}$ for the complete RS24 source Gram in the
$x$-coefficient basis and $G_{W,x}$ for the corresponding
original target Gram. Reserve $G_U=\Phi^*\langle\ ,\ \rangle_U\Phi$
for its earlier marked meaning; these two matrices are related by
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 G_U=Z^*G_{U,x}Z,\quad G_X=K^*G_UK,\qquad
 G=Z^*G_{W,x}Z,\quad G_Y=K^*GK,\\
 \mathbb H_U=Q_x^*\operatorname{diag}(G_{U,x},G_{U,x})Q_x
 =\begin{pmatrix}
 G_X+E^*G_UE&E^*G_UO\\O^*G_UE&O^*G_UO
 \end{pmatrix},\\
 \mathbb H_W=Q_x^*\operatorname{diag}(G_{W,x},G_{W,x})Q_x
 =\begin{pmatrix}
 G_Y+E^*GE&E^*GO\\O^*GE&O^*GO
 \end{pmatrix}.
 \end{gathered}\tag{MR4}
\end{equation}
The first line follows from $C_UZ=\Phi$, $C_UZK=X$ and
their target counterparts, proved in MR3. Multiplication
of the complete block matrices then proves every remaining
entry. In particular the mixed terms $E^*G_UO$ and $O^*G_UE$
are present; neither parity orthogonality nor a diagonal
marked metric was assumed. The full moment and Gamma formula
for $G_{U,x}$, its inverse and all phases is RS21--26.

Let $\Theta_w=M_{2t^3}/c$ be RS3 with its exact phase
$c=(1+i)/\sqrt2$, and put
\begin{equation}
 \Theta_{{\rm mark},w}=Q_x^{-1}\Theta_wQ_x,\qquad
 \Theta_{{\rm mark},w}^{-1}=Q_x^{-1}\Theta_w^{-1}Q_x.
 \tag{MR5}
\end{equation}
All maps in this identity act on the same eight signed
coordinates as RD19. Their physical original-source and
target forms are precisely $\mathcal J_U\Theta_{{\rm mark},w}
\mathcal J_U^{-1}$ and $\mathcal J_W\Theta_{{\rm mark},w}
\mathcal J_W^{-1}$. MR3 proves their intertwining by
$T_A\oplus T_A$ in both directions.

Here is the full singular-value argument including the metric.
For any invertible $Q$, positive $G_8$, and matrix $A$, set
$H=Q^*G_8Q$ and $A_Q=Q^{-1}AQ$. Direct inversion and
multiplication give
\begin{equation}
 H^{-1}A_Q^*HA_Q
       =Q^{-1}G_8^{-1}A^*G_8AQ.
 \tag{MR6}
\end{equation}
Both sides therefore have the same eigenvalues. Those eigenvalues
are the squared singular values in their respective positive
metrics, because conjugation by the positive square root of
the metric gives the usual adjoint product. Thus every singular
value is retained. Exterior norms are retained as well: the
Gram of wedges is the determinant of the individual pairings,
and an isometry preserves each of these pairings. With MR4,
this proves, for every $w>0$ and $0\le j\le8$,
\begin{equation}
 \left\|\bigwedge^j\Theta_{{\rm mark},w}^{-1}\right\|_{\mathbb H_U}
 =\left\|\bigwedge^j\Theta_w^{-1}\right\|_{
             \operatorname{diag}(G_{U,x},G_{U,x})}
 =\left\|\bigwedge^j\Theta_{U,w}^{-1}\right\|_{U\oplus U}.
 \tag{MR7}
\end{equation}
Consequently RS14--19 give the exact marked constants, with
the explicit original-source substitution RS24--25. The
target statement follows by the same proved identity with
$G_{W,x}$. No condition-number estimate replaces either
source or target constants in MR7.

\section{The complete nilpotent flag of the inverse pole}
Keep $g=\gamma^2>0$, $X_0,N,H_0$ of RS5, and let
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 v_1=(g,0,1,0)^{\mathsf T},\quad
 v_2=(0,g,0,1)^{\mathsf T},\quad
 V=\operatorname{span}\{v_1,v_2\}=\operatorname{im}N=\ker N,\\
 \mathscr L=\lim_{w\downarrow0}w\Theta_w^{-1}
 =c\begin{pmatrix}0&H_0/(32g^2)\\(gI+N)/(32g^3)&0\end{pmatrix},\\
 \Theta_0=\frac2c\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\H_0&0\end{pmatrix}.
 \end{gathered}\tag{MR8}
\end{equation}
The first two columns of $N$ are $v_1,v_2$; its remaining
columns are $-gv_1,-gv_2$. They are independent, and $N^2=0$,
proving both descriptions of $V$. Its polynomials are exactly
$x^2+g$ and $x(x^2+g)$, with their original coefficients.
Since $X_0$ commutes with $N$ and is invertible,
$\operatorname{im}H_0=\ker H_0=V$ and $H_0N=NH_0=0$.
Multiplication of the unchanged matrices gives
\begin{equation}
 \mathscr L^2=\frac{c^2}{2^{10}g^4}
            \begin{pmatrix}H_0&0\\0&H_0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \mathscr L^3=\frac{c^3}{2^{15}g^6}
            \begin{pmatrix}0&0\\H_0&0\end{pmatrix}
       =-\frac{\Theta_0}{2^{16}g^6},\qquad
 \mathscr L^4=0.
 \tag{MR9}
\end{equation}
For the first product use $H_0(gI+N)=gH_0$; for the second
also use $H_0^2=0$. In comparing with $\Theta_0$ the factor
is $c^4/2^{16}g^6=-1/2^{16}g^6$, so the phase is essential.

The complete image and kernel flags, in even then odd order, are
\begin{equation}
 \begin{array}{c|c|c|c}
 j&\operatorname{im}\mathscr L^j&\ker\mathscr L^j&
     \operatorname{rank}\mathscr L^j\\\hline
 0&\C^4\oplus\C^4&0&8\\
 1&V\oplus\C^4&0\oplus V&6\\
 2&V\oplus V&V\oplus V&4\\
 3&0\oplus V&V\oplus\C^4&2\\
 4&0&\C^4\oplus\C^4&0
 \end{array}
 \tag{MR10}
\end{equation}
Indeed $(gI+N)^{-1}=g^{-1}I-g^{-2}N$ since $N^2=0$,
whereas $H_0$ has precisely image and kernel $V$. This proves
the first row of nontrivial subspaces directly from MR8;
MR9 proves the others. In particular
$\ker\Theta_0=V\oplus\C^4$ and
$\operatorname{im}\Theta_0=0\oplus V$.

There are two explicit complete chains. Let $e_0,e_1$
denote the first two cubic coefficient vectors, and set
$u_j=(e_j,0)$, for $j=0,1$. Without rescaling any vector they are
\begin{equation}
 \begin{split}
 u_j&=(e_j,0),\\
 \mathscr L u_j&=\left(0,\frac{c(gI+N)e_j}{32g^3}\right),\\
 \mathscr L^2u_j&=\left(\frac{c^2H_0e_j}{1024g^4},0\right),\\
 \mathscr L^3u_j&=\left(0,\frac{c^3H_0e_j}{32768g^6}\right),\\
 H_0e_0&=-2v_2,\qquad H_0e_1=2gv_1.
 \end{split}\tag{MR11}
\end{equation}
Both last vectors are independent. In any linear relation
between these eight vectors, applying $\mathscr L^3$ first
forces the coefficients of $u_0,u_1$ to vanish. Applying
$\mathscr L^2$ then forces the coefficients of
$\mathscr L u_0,\mathscr L u_1$ to vanish; applying
$\mathscr L$ and then using the independence of the last
vectors eliminates the remaining coefficients. Thus the
ordered columns
$(\mathscr L^3u_0,\mathscr L^2u_0,\mathscr L u_0,u_0,
  \mathscr L^3u_1,\mathscr L^2u_1,\mathscr L u_1,u_1)$
are a basis and exhibit exactly two blocks of length four.

Every vector and subspace in this flag returns to the original
marked and physical spaces by the explicit isomorphisms
\begin{equation}
 \mathscr L_{\rm mark}=Q_x^{-1}\mathscr LQ_x,
 \quad \operatorname{im}\mathscr L_{\rm mark}^j
       =Q_x^{-1}\operatorname{im}\mathscr L^j,
 \quad \ker\mathscr L_{\rm mark}^j
       =Q_x^{-1}\ker\mathscr L^j,
 \quad \mathcal J_UQ_x^{-1}=\mathcal C_U.
 \tag{MR12}
\end{equation}
The corresponding target map is $\mathcal J_WQ_x^{-1}
=\mathcal C_W$. Applying $T_A\oplus T_A$ to the source
chain gives the target chain term by term. MR2 supplies the
entire inverse, so no marked state or phase is omitted.
For any chain column matrix $B_j$, its actual source Gram
is exactly $B_j^*\operatorname{diag}(G_{U,x},G_{U,x})B_j$,
which equals $(Q_x^{-1}B_j)^*\mathbb H_U(Q_x^{-1}B_j)$
by MR4. This also proves the metric statement for every flag.

\section{Exact finite-parameter inverse bounds with the original metric}
The singular asymptotes admit a fully evaluated error term.
For every $w>0$, the matrices RS4 satisfy
\begin{equation}
 \Theta_w^{-1}=\frac{\mathscr L}{w}
       +c\begin{pmatrix}0&A_w\\B_w&0\end{pmatrix},
 \tag{MR13}
\end{equation}
where every entry of the remainder is
\begin{equation}
 A_w=\begin{pmatrix}
 0&-\frac1{16g}&0&-\frac{2g+w}{16g}\\
 \frac1{2(g+w)^2}&0&-\frac1{16g}&0\\
 0&0&0&\frac1{16g}\\
 \frac{3g+w}{16g^2(g+w)^2}&0&0&0
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad
 B_w=\begin{pmatrix}
 -\frac{3g+w}{16g^2(g+w)^2}&0&0&0\\
 0&-\frac{3g+w}{16g^2(g+w)^2}&0&0\\
 -\frac{2g+w}{32g^3(g+w)^2}&0&0&0\\
 0&-\frac{2g+w}{32g^3(g+w)^2}&0&0
 \end{pmatrix}.
 \tag{MR14}
\end{equation}
For a direct verification put $a=g-w$, $p=(g+w)^2$.
The exact inverse of RS2 is
\begin{equation}
 X_w^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}
 0&1&0&0\\-2a/p&0&1&0\\0&0&0&1\\-1/p&0&0&0
 \end{pmatrix}.
 \tag{MR15}
\end{equation}
Multiplying both products with $X_w$ proves this inverse.
Subtracting $H_0/(32g^2w)$ from
$(X_w+aX_w^{-1})/(16gw)$ gives exactly $A_w$.
Subtracting $(gI+N)/(32g^3w)$ from
$-X_w^{-2}/(32gw)$ gives exactly $B_w$.
These are entrywise calculations and prove MR13--14, including
the factors of $g$, $w$ and $c$. Every entry extends continuously
to $w=0$ for the unchanged $g>0$.

For the original source set $G_0=G_{U,x}$ with its complete
RS24--25 evaluation, and define the following explicit finite
quantities:
\begin{equation}
 \begin{split}
 e(w)^2&=\operatorname{tr}(G_0^{-1}A_w^*G_0A_w)
            +\operatorname{tr}(G_0^{-1}B_w^*G_0B_w),\\
 b(w)^2&=4\operatorname{tr}(G_0^{-1}(H_w^2)^*G_0H_w^2)
            +4\operatorname{tr}(G_0^{-1}H_w^*G_0H_w),\\
 p_j(w)&=\max\{0,c_j-we(w)\},\qquad
 q_j(w)=c_j+we(w),\quad 1\le j\le6 .
 \end{split}\tag{MR16}
\end{equation}
The $c_j$ are the six explicitly evaluated RS14 constants
for this $G_0$. All entries in MR16 are displayed in
RS2, RS24--25 and MR14. The two traces in each line are
sums of squares in the original positive metric. Hence $e(w)$
is the full Hilbert--Schmidt norm of the remainder and $b(w)$
is that of $\Theta_w$. Both are finite, and $b(w)>0$ for
every $w>0$. The phase has modulus one and remains present
in MR13 even though it cancels from these norms.

The variational characterization of singular values gives
$|\sigma_j(A)-\sigma_j(B)|\le\|A-B\|$: apply the triangle
inequality on each subspace in the minimum-maximum formula,
and then reverse $A,B$. Apply this to $w\Theta_w^{-1}$ and
$\mathscr L$. Its first six singular values are $c_j$, and
its last two are zero. The operator norm is bounded by the
Hilbert--Schmidt norm, so MR13 proves
\begin{equation}
 p_j(w)\le w\sigma_j(\Theta_{{\rm mark},w}^{-1})\le q_j(w)
 \quad(1\le j\le6),\qquad
 b(w)^{-1}\le\sigma_j(\Theta_{{\rm mark},w}^{-1})\le e(w)
 \quad(j=7,8).
 \tag{MR17}
\end{equation}
For the lower bound on the last two, the smallest singular
value of an inverse is the reciprocal of the largest singular
value of the original. This follows by inverting orthonormal
singular bases. The latter largest value is at most $b(w)$.
MR7 transfers all these inequalities to the actual marked
metric without any comparison loss.

Multiplying the decreasing singular values yields, for every
$w>0$, the complete inverse exterior bounds
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac{\prod_{j=1}^r p_j(w)}{w^r}
 &\le\|\bigwedge^r\Theta_{{\rm mark},w}^{-1}\|_{\mathbb H_U}
 \le\frac{\prod_{j=1}^r q_j(w)}{w^r},&&1\le r\le6,\\
 \frac{\prod_{j=1}^6p_j(w)}{w^6b(w)^{r-6}}
 &\le\|\bigwedge^r\Theta_{{\rm mark},w}^{-1}\|_{\mathbb H_U}
 \le\frac{\prod_{j=1}^6q_j(w)e(w)^{r-6}}{w^6},&&r=7,8,\\
 \|\bigwedge^8\Theta_{{\rm mark},w}^{-1}\|_{\mathbb H_U}
 &=\frac1{2^{32}g^6w^6(g+w)^6}.
 \end{aligned}\tag{MR18}
\end{equation}
Rank zero has norm one. Continuity of MR14 at zero implies
$we(w)\to0$; all $c_j$ are positive by RS8 and RS13.
Thus the lower bounds on the first six factors become positive
near zero and approach their exact constants. RS19 supplies
the two remaining exact leading constants. Equations MR7 and
MR18 are the full original-metric conclusion for the completed
trace-to-residue operation, at finite parameters as well as in
the limit.

The loss occurs in the explicit multiplication $2t^3/c$, whose
critical rank is two and whose inverse has the chains MR11.
The original conductor itself is the fixed isomorphism FC23--24.
MR3, MR5 and MR12 prove the exact relation to that isomorphism;
they do not replace its moment $\mu_v$ or change its order.

\section{Uniform original-metric error and the two finite singular values}
The exact remainder MR14 also gives bounds with constants
independent of $w$ on a specified interval. Keep $g>0$ unchanged,
put $G_0=G_{U,x}$ with all its original entries from RS24--25,
and define
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \kappa_{G_0}=
   \sqrt{\operatorname{tr}G_0\operatorname{tr}G_0^{-1}},\\
 B(g)=\frac9{256}+\frac3{256g^2}+\frac1{4g^4}
             +\frac3{16g^6}+\frac9{512g^8},\\
 K_{G_0}=\kappa_{G_0}\sqrt{B(g)},\qquad
 D_{G_0}=\kappa_{G_0}\sqrt{4g^4+216g^2+12}.
 \end{gathered}
 \tag{MR19}
\end{equation}
All quantities are finite positive expressions in the retained
original moments, Gamma weights and $g$. For an endomorphism
$A$ of the cubic coefficient space,
\[
 \|A\|_{G_0}
 =\|G_0^{1/2}AG_0^{-1/2}\|_2
 \le\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(G_0)\lambda_{\max}(G_0^{-1})}
                 \,\|A\|_{\mathrm F}
 \le\kappa_{G_0}\|A\|_{\mathrm F}.
\]
The first equality follows from the isometric map
$z\mapsto G_0^{1/2}z$. The first inequality follows by
submultiplication and the bound of the operator norm by the
sum of squared matrix entries. The second follows because
each positive eigenvalue is at most the sum of all eigenvalues.
For $\operatorname{diag}(G_0,G_0)$ the largest eigenvalue and
the largest inverse eigenvalue are unchanged, so the same
$\kappa_{G_0}$ applies to the full eight-dimensional operator.
The matrices $G_0$ and $G_0^{-1}$ remain those in RS24--25.

For $0<w\le g$, the absolute value of each entry of $A_w,B_w$
in MR14 is bounded by the corresponding entry below:
\begin{equation}
 A^\flat=
 \begin{pmatrix}
 0&\frac1{16g}&0&\frac3{16}\\
 \frac1{2g^2}&0&\frac1{16g}&0\\
 0&0&0&\frac1{16g}\\
 \frac1{4g^3}&0&0&0
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad
 B^\flat=
 \begin{pmatrix}
 \frac1{4g^3}&0&0&0\\
 0&\frac1{4g^3}&0&0\\
 \frac3{32g^4}&0&0&0\\
 0&\frac3{32g^4}&0&0
 \end{pmatrix}.
 \tag{MR20}
\end{equation}
To verify every bound, use $g+w\ge g$, $2g+w\le3g$ and
$3g+w\le4g$. The entries $\pm1/(16g)$ are unchanged,
the $(0,3)$ entry of $A_w$ is at most $3/16$, its $(1,0)$
entry is at most $1/(2g^2)$, and its $(3,0)$ entry is at most
$1/(4g^3)$. The first two nonzero entries of $B_w$ have
the same last bound, and the remaining two are at most
$3/(32g^4)$. This accounts for every nonzero entry.
Their squared sums are
\[
 \|A^\flat\|_{\mathrm F}^2
 =\frac9{256}+\frac3{256g^2}+\frac1{4g^4}+\frac1{16g^6},
 \qquad
 \|B^\flat\|_{\mathrm F}^2
 =\frac1{8g^6}+\frac9{512g^8}.
\]
Together they equal $B(g)$ in MR19. Since $|c|=1$, MR13
and the proved metric inequality yield the uniform estimate
\begin{equation}
 \left\|\Theta_w^{-1}-\frac{\mathscr L}{w}
       \right\|_{\operatorname{diag}(G_0,G_0)}
 \le K_{G_0},\qquad 0<w\le g.
 \tag{MR21}
\end{equation}
The phase $c$ remains the exact one in MR13; only its
modulus enters this inequality. MR6--7 give the identical
inequality after conjugation by the actual $Q_x$, in
$\mathbb H_U$, with
$\mathscr L_{\rm mark}=Q_x^{-1}\mathscr LQ_x$.

We also need the finite nonzero singular values of the
forward derivative multiplier, rather than only the six
values of the inverse pole. Subtracting the two full
matrices RS2 gives
\begin{equation}
 \frac{H_w-H_0}{w}=
 \begin{pmatrix}
 0&2(2g+w)&0&2(-g^2+gw+w^2)\\
 2&0&2(2g+w)&0\\
 0&-2&0&2(6g-w)\\
 0&0&-2&0
 \end{pmatrix}.
 \tag{MR22}
\end{equation}
This is an identity for every $w>0$, obtained by multiplying
the companion polynomial in RS2 before subtracting its value
at zero. On $0\le w\le g$,
\[
 -g^2\le -g^2+gw+w^2\le g^2.
\]
The lower inequality is $gw+w^2\ge0$; the upper is
$(g-w)(2g+w)\ge0$. Hence the absolute entries of MR22 are
bounded by
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}
 0&6g&0&2g^2\\2&0&6g&0\\0&2&0&12g\\0&0&2&0
 \end{pmatrix}.
\]
Its squared entry sum is exactly $4g^4+216g^2+12$.
Applying the metric estimate from MR19 proves
\begin{equation}
 \|H_w-H_0\|_{G_0}\le wD_{G_0},\qquad
 |\sigma_j^{G_0}(H_w)-\eta_j|\le wD_{G_0},
 \quad j=1,2,\quad 0<w\le g.
 \tag{MR23}
\end{equation}
The second inequality is the same singular-value variational
argument used in MR17; $\eta_1\ge\eta_2>0$ are the two
complete RS8 values in the same $G_0$.

Let $c_6>0$ be the smallest of the six explicitly specified
RS14 constants. Define the following positive threshold:
\begin{equation}
 w_*=\min\left\{
 g,\ \frac{\eta_2}{2D_{G_0}},\
 \frac{c_6}{2(K_{G_0}+1/\eta_2)}
 \right\},\qquad \epsilon_w=wD_{G_0}.
 \tag{MR24}
\end{equation}
This retains the original $w=\delta^2$ and $g=\gamma^2$;
every entry of the threshold is already a finite expression
in the original source data. For $0<w\le w_*$,
$\epsilon_w\le\eta_2/2$. By MR23 the first two singular
values of $H_w$ therefore lie in the positive intervals
$[\eta_j-\epsilon_w,\eta_j+\epsilon_w]$.
Their inverse values $1/(2\sigma_j^{G_0}(H_w))$ are both
at most $1/\eta_2$.

The upper-right block of $\Theta_w^{-1}$ is
$cH_w^{-1}/2$, while its lower-left block is
$cH_w^{-2}/2$. Because the two summands have the same
original Gram $G_0$, their singular spectra combine to
the singular spectrum of the whole inverse, as proved in
RS15. Thus these two bounded reciprocal values occur among
the eight actual singular values. On the other hand MR21
and the variational inequality give
\[
 \sigma_6(\Theta_w^{-1})
 \ge c_6/w-K_{G_0}
 \ge K_{G_0}+2/\eta_2>1/\eta_2.
\]
There are therefore exactly six larger values before those
two reciprocal values. This proves their indices, including
possible equality between $\eta_1$ and $\eta_2$:
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \sigma_7(\Theta_{{\rm mark},w}^{-1})
   &=\frac1{2\sigma_2^{G_0}(H_w)},&
 \left|\sigma_7-\frac1{2\eta_2}\right|
   &\le\frac{wD_{G_0}}{\eta_2^2},\\
 \sigma_8(\Theta_{{\rm mark},w}^{-1})
   &=\frac1{2\sigma_1^{G_0}(H_w)},&
 \left|\sigma_8-\frac1{2\eta_1}\right|
   &\le\frac{wD_{G_0}}{\eta_1^2},\\
 \left|\sigma_j(\Theta_{{\rm mark},w}^{-1})-\frac{c_j}{w}\right|
   &\le K_{G_0},&&1\le j\le6,\qquad 0<w\le w_*.
 \end{aligned}
 \tag{MR25}
\end{equation}
For the finite errors, if $|s-\eta|\le\epsilon_w$ then
\[
 \left|\frac1{2s}-\frac1{2\eta}\right|
 =\frac{|s-\eta|}{2\eta s}
 \le\frac{\epsilon_w}{2\eta(\eta-\epsilon_w)}
 \le\frac{\epsilon_w}{\eta^2}.
\]
The last step uses $\epsilon_w\le\eta_2/2\le\eta/2$.
MR7 transfers both the indexing and inequalities to the
full marked metric, retaining its $E,O$ cross terms.

These estimates also give uniformly positive finite-parameter
exterior bounds. For $0<w\le w_*$ put
\[
 P_r^\pm(w)=\prod_{j=1}^r(c_j\pm wK_{G_0}),
 \qquad 1\le r\le6.
\]
Every minus factor is positive: MR24 gives
$c_j/w-K_{G_0}\ge c_6/w-K_{G_0}>1/\eta_2>0$.
For $1\le r\le6$ and $s=1,2$ the complete bounds are
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac{P_r^-(w)}{w^r}
 &\le\|\wedge^r\Theta_{{\rm mark},w}^{-1}\|_{\mathbb H_U}
 \le\frac{P_r^+(w)}{w^r},\\
 \frac{P_6^-(w)}
 {w^6\,2^s\prod_{j=1}^s(\eta_{3-j}+\epsilon_w)}
 &\le\|\wedge^{6+s}\Theta_{{\rm mark},w}^{-1}\|_{\mathbb H_U}
 \le
 \frac{P_6^+(w)}
 {w^6\,2^s\prod_{j=1}^s(\eta_{3-j}-\epsilon_w)} .
 \end{aligned}
 \tag{MR26}
\end{equation}
Every denominator on both sides is positive by MR24.
The first line multiplies the first six inequalities
MR25. The second also uses the exact reciprocal intervals
$[1/(2(\eta_j+\epsilon_w)),1/(2(\eta_j-\epsilon_w))]$
proved above, in their established index order.
Rank zero still has norm one. At rank eight the exact
determinant in MR18 remains the sharper identity
$1/(2^{32}g^6w^6(g+w)^6)$. Thus MR25--26 control both
finite singular directions and all six divergent directions,
with the original source Gram evaluated through every
moment and with its complete marked return.

\section{Global signed duality and the complete ES family}


The proposed eight sections were supplied in the received
ramification continuation, Section 2 \cite{RamificationReceived}. Their
even basis and odd coefficient module have established human
provenance: specialize Melanie Matchett Wood's equations
\texttt{E:basis} and \texttt{E:Isbasis} to
$(f_0,f_1,f_2,f_3,f_4)=(A,1,B,C,D)$ \cite{WoodBinary}.
Wood credits the even basis to Birch--Merriman and its
multiplication to Nakagawa; her equation \texttt{E:mult}
is their explicit ring law. The form here is primitive
because $f_1=1$, and Wood's proposition \texttt{P:Iinvert}
identifies its odd coefficient module with $I_f^{-1}$.
We now prove directly that the proposed sections are a
global basis of the actual signed extension from FS2--10,
including its original sign convention, and calculate its
entire multiplication tensor and trace. No theorem about
a different choice of sign coordinate is substituted.

\section{Regular sections in both original root charts}
Let $\mathfrak B=\C[A,B,C,D]$ and retain
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 h(r)=Ar^4+r^3+Br^2+Cr+D,\qquad
 k(v)=A+v+Bv^2+Cv^3+Dv^4,\\
 h(r)=0,\quad t^2=h'(r),\qquad
 k(v)=0,\quad\theta^2=-k'(v),\\
 v=1/r,\quad\theta=-t/r,\qquad
 e=(1,Ar,Ar^2+r,Ar^3+r^2+Br),\\
 o=t(A,Ar+1,Ar^2+r+B,Ar^3+r^2+Br+C).
 \end{gathered}
 \tag{GB1}
\end{equation}
The completed cover $X$ is obtained by these two charts
and exact inverses $r=1/v$, $t=-\theta/v$.
This is the finite cover constructed in FS2--10
\cite{Fable037}; in particular the reciprocal original factor
is exactly $t=1/a$. The gluing identity follows by
differentiating $k(v)=v^4h(1/v)$ at a root.

On the infinity chart the same sections are the polynomials
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 e_0=1,\qquad e_1=-1-Bv-Cv^2-Dv^3,\qquad
 e_2=-B-Cv-Dv^2,\qquad e_3=-C-Dv,\\
 (o_0,o_1,o_2,o_3)=(-e_1,-e_2,-e_3,D)\theta.
 \end{gathered}
 \tag{GB2}
\end{equation}
For instance $A/v=-1-Bv-Cv^2-Dv^3$ follows by dividing
the root equation on the overlap; its right side is regular
on the whole infinity chart. The same relation gives
$(A+v)/v^2=-B-Cv-Dv^2$ and
$(A+v+Bv^2)/v^3=-C-Dv$.
Multiplying these expressions by $t=-\theta/v$ proves
the odd formulas, including the last value $D\theta$.
They agree on the overlap and so define all eight global
